# Decisions — Determinatives as nouns
<!-- SUMMARY: Project decisions, including structured methodological records and the completed paragraph-flow repairs · status: active · updated: 2026-09-22 -->

2026-07-07 — The approximative `or so` construction is treated as suggestive evidence only, not as a primary category diagnostic. Added a numbered example contrasting `fifteen or so minutes` with `ready by Tuesday or so`, framed on `CGEL` terms as `so` coordinating with a cardinal DP in one case and with a proper-noun-headed NP in the other. Reason: the construction supports the idea that DP and NP occupy adjacent territory inside a broader nominal domain, but its idiomatic and scalar restrictions prevent it from bearing the category argument.

2026-05-06 — Submission package built and committed (4d0e508). Sent to: **Andrea Sims** (sims.120@osu.edu) judged the best topical fit on the editorial board (paradigm structure / network morphology / category-internal organization, US institution). Huyghe and Arkadiev are the alternates if she redirects. Reason: the journal does not use a portal; submission is by email to one editor. The package in `submission/` contains: `Determinatives_as_nouns_anon.pdf` (anonymized), `AI_Disclosure_Form_completed.docx` (EUP form, five Yes rows), `Determinatives_as_nouns_source.zip` (anon source with stripped bibs, only on request), `email-to-sims.md` and `SUBMISSION.md`.

[Title removed for anonymous review]` stubs (`anon2021dist`, `anon2021matrix`, `anon2026gender`, `anon2026numerals`, `anon2025cgelbank`). `main-anon.tex` swaps cite keys to anon* and rephrases prose mentions of "Reynolds" (e.g., "Reynolds analyses cardinals" → "That work analyses cardinals"). The source zip strips 51 Reynolds-authored entries from the central `references.bib` (key match `reynolds*` / `pullumreynolds*` / `nefdtreynolds*` and author/editor field match for "Reynolds, Brett") plus all comment lines mentioning "Reynolds" or "Brett". 2026-05-06 — Keywords finalized at five (journal cap): `determinatives, lexical categories, noun, category status, English`. Reason: prior expansion to seven (added "pronouns" and "Cambridge Grammar of English") exceeded the 3–5 cap. "Word classes" was replaced with "lexical categories" to match the paper's CGEL-internal terminology and to avoid the term appearing nowhere else in the manuscript.

2026-05-06 — Hamming-distance metric named explicitly in §5; verification script committed at `notes/distance-verification.py`. Per-item distances (24.8 / 26.2 / 25.2 / 29.0 / 21.8 / 36.8 / 28.8 / 30.0) reproduce within ~1 unit on the 138-form by 155-feature matrix using core determinatives = demonstratives + quantifiers and sampled pronouns = personal + dummy/locative pronouns. Footnote at line 28 explains the 232 → 155 filtering (singleton features dropped; the LingBuzz matrix is the filtered version). Reason: `/proofread` flagged the per-item numbers as unverified; the metric was unstated in prose and the matrix-size mismatch (paper said 232; LingBuzz CSV has 155) needed reconciling.

2026-05-06 — Saturation argument strengthened with generic/proverb examples (*Many are called, few are chosen*; *All is not lost*; *Enough is enough*). Reason: ellipsis or antecedent-recovery analysis cannot apply where there is no antecedent, so these cases put the structural-saturation discriminator on a firmer footing than `I'll take some` (where anaphoric recovery is at least available). Inserted in §3 after the existing Mod–Head saturation discriminator.

2026-04-29 — Journal target: *Word Structure*.

2026-04-29 — Payne, Huddleston & Pullum 2010 ("The distribution and category status of adjectives and adverbs," *Word Structure*) promoted to lead methodological foil, replacing Pullum & Miller 2022 in that role. Reason: PHP 2010 is the structurally closest precedent — same journal, same diagnostic shape (distributional category-status argument), same author lineage, and it already handles *few*, *any*, *some*, *nothing* via fused-head determinative analysis. Reviewers familiar with the journal will read the paper as a sequel to PHP 2010, not as analogous work. Frame the proposal as "preserving PHP's local categorial continuity (anti-pronoun move for *few*, *any*, *some*) while revising the supercategory assignment (determinative → sub-category of noun)." Pullum & Miller 2022 stays in the literature engagement but moves from lead foil to one CGEL-side defence among several. How to apply: add a "Payne–Huddleston–Pullum precedent" subsection (probably end of §1 or in §6); rework §7's adverbial-modification defence to address the *hardly any* reintroduction problem explicitly; sharpen the "supercategory-vs-coordinate-category" distinction to answer PHP's distribution-per-se warning. See `notes/word-structure-revision-strategy.md` for full revision plan. Source: `literature/Payne, Huddleston, Pullum - 2010 - The distribution and category status of adjectives and adverbs(2) copy.md`.

2026-04-22 — Preprint posted to LingBuzz as [009939](https://lingbuzz.net/lingbuzz/009939) "Determinatives as nouns in English." PORTFOLIO.md, website publications.html, CV (main.tex + main.pdf) all updated. No journal target yet.

2026-04-20 — Scope: English-only. No cross-linguistic typology claim. Any Phase-5 typology reading (Wiltschko 2014, Rijkhoff, Dryer) is out of scope.

2026-04-20 — Numerals are not a fifth noun sub-category. Cardinals are absorbed into the existing taxonomy per Reynolds 2026 (*EL&L*): cardinals are both determinatives and (proper or common) nouns; ordinals are adjectives; fractionals are nouns. The determinative category stays a small closed list. Reason: the numerals paper already does the categorial work; creating a fifth sub-cluster would double-count.

2026-04-20 — HPC is de-emphasised. Not load-bearing in the argument; possibly framing. Reason: the proposal stands as a CGEL-internal taxonomic move without requiring the HPC machinery.

2026-04-20 — Literature engagement is targeted, not exhaustive. Core opponents: Pullum & Miller 2022 (CGEL-side defence of separation), Postal 1969 / Hudson 2004 / Anderson (unification moves in the wrong direction), Abney 1987 (DP, foil not engagement). Reason: tighter paper, clearer contrast.

2026-04-20 (rev.) — Framing updated from "novel proposal" to "architectural extension of the Spinillo/Van Eynde dissolve-the-Det move, with a specific commitment to all-under-Noun." Reason: Spinillo 2000/2004 and Van Eynde 2003 both argue against Det as a standalone category; Brett's move is the specific fourfold-Noun taxonomy. Must differentiate explicitly.

2026-04-20 — Pullum & Wilson 1977 "Autonomous syntax and the analysis of auxiliaries" (*Language*) becomes the load-bearing analogy for dissolving Det into N: AUX was dissolved into V by GPSG/CGEL-adjacent work; the same move applies to Det → N. Van Eynde 2003 uses this precedent explicitly for determiners. Reason: stronger than *to*-as-auxiliary (minority view), published Pullum precedent, in-house.

2026-04-20 — **Checkpoint 1 (go).** Framing is locked. Primary engagement: Pullum & Miller 2022, Van Eynde 2003, Spinillo 2000/2004. One-paragraph differentiation from Hudson 2004, Postal 1969, Anderson 1997. Foil: Abney 1987. HPC de-emphasised. Proceed to Phase 1 (CGEL/SIEG and self-corpus consolidation). Reason: the Phase 0 reads (P&M close read, Reynolds 2026, prior-art search) converge on a coherent architectural proposal — extension of the dissolve-Det tradition with the specific fourfold-Noun commitment, supported by the aux-as-V analogy. No substantive reframing needed; just explicit differentiation from the prior-art proposals.

2026-04-20 — **Checkpoint 2 (load-bearing phenomena).** The paper's core payoff argument is built on two phenomena that pair naturally: (i) **fused-head reanalysis** — *I'll take some* becomes ordinary nominal headedness, eliminating the fusion-of-functions apparatus that Payne, Huddleston & Pullum (2007) develop; (iii) **NP:Det uniformity** — the Det function is uniformly filled by an NP, collapsing the current CGEL disjunction "Det = DP or genitive NP" into a single category. Reason: these are the two places where CGEL Ch 5 machinery creaks, and both simplify under the det-as-noun move. (i) is the strongest payoff because fusion-of-functions is substantial theoretical machinery explicitly defended over ellipsis and null-head alternatives; eliminating it is a measurable analytical gain.

2026-04-20 — **Supporting phenomena, not central.** (ii) **Stacking restriction** as functional ("one Det per NP") rather than categorial — already implicit in CGEL, so this is expository cleanup. (iv) ***the many people* alternation** as Det vs Mod of the same determinative noun — Brett's original thought; still useful as supporting data but cannot be load-bearing because it creates a puzzle: if determinatives are nouns, why do they take AdvP modifiers (*almost every, hardly any*) while common nouns take AdjP modifiers (*a genuine professional*)? Answer to handle in paper: sub-cluster-specific combinatorics, consistent with PHP 2010:13-14 generalisation that *few* is a determinative in both *few doctors* and *the few doctors*. Sub-cluster modifier-selection asymmetry is evidence for the sub-cluster distinction, not against the superordinate Noun.

2026-04-20 — ***The/a* steelman empirically grounded.** Lyons 1999 (diachronic/typological), PHP 2007:22-23 (lexical defectiveness of *every, no*), and CGEL 371-2 (explicit acknowledgment of *the/a* defectiveness) converge: articles are the maximally grammaticalised, phonologically weak, semantically minimal, cross-linguistically non-universal limit of the determinative sub-cluster. Paper treats them as the defective limit of the sub-cluster, parallel to defective proper nouns (*\*the John*; place names that reject articles). Reason: this is the worst case for the proposal, so the paper must handle it explicitly; the empirical support lets us handle it without special-pleading.

2026-04-22 — The core argument is now explicitly two-stage. Partitives establish determinatives as head-like pivots in matrix NPs independently of the fusion question; the simple one-word cases are a second-stage argument about whether that headhood is canonical or fused. Reason: this separates the strongest headhood evidence from the more controversial anti-fusion claim.

2026-04-22 — `Mine` and `Kim's` are not the load-bearing anti-fusion model. They are retained as evidence that fusion is compatible with nounhood/pronominality, but the main headhood case should not lean on them. Reason: the possessive subsystem is structurally mixed and weakens any attempt to use `mine` as a clean simple-Head control.

2026-04-22 — The paper rejects `CGEL`'s `subject--determiner` analysis for its own purposes. Prenominal genitives are treated simply as genitive NPs or genitive pronouns in Det function. Reason: the `subject-` component encodes a semantic/thematic intuition, not a syntactic function the paper needs.

2026-04-22 — Named review-board consensus on the current full draft: `Revise & Resubmit`. Main pressure points are the instability between the nounhood claim and the anti-fusion criterion, the under-demonstrated simplification at the determiner slot, the handling of `the/a`, and incomplete differentiation from the strongest rivals (Spinillo, A/N-split, Hudson-style unification). Reason: six-reviewer `gpt-5.4` board converged strongly enough to treat these as the live revision priorities.

2026-04-22 — Compatibility framing pivot. Criterion at line 103 rewritten: productivity + non-parasitic-residue replaces the "rather than reached via fusion" clause. Fusion is a separate constructional question; the categorial conclusion holds either way. Reason: review board converged on the criterion fighting the paper's strongest insight; six reviewers all praised the compatibility framing once it was made central.

2026-04-22 — Section title changes. §2 "The other sub-categories of Noun" (was "existing"); §3 "From Noun to determiner function" (was "Bridge from Noun to Determiner" — Det is a function, not a category). Reason: terminological precision (existing was vague; Determiner capitalised read as category).

2026-04-22 — Five Pullum & Wilson rhetorical moves applied. (1) "Even if you grant" framing in abstract and §1; (2) category-inventory reduction appeal in Conclusion; (3) cross-cutting features (gender data) framed as P&W §1.2-style argument in §7; (4) "Criteria for keeping determinative primary" subsection in §4 listing the rival's positive case and showing each consideration is consistent with sub-category-of-N; (5) "the categorial verdict does not" closing-style refrain in three places. Reason: P&W 1977 provides the closest argumentative template (AUX → V); applying its rhetorical structure systematically.

2026-04-22 — Refrain (ring composition): "Fusion may vary by construction; the categorial verdict does not" appears at end of §1 compatibility paragraph, end of §4.1 reanalysis, and (variant) at the very end of the Conclusion. Reason: structural rhetorical reinforcement; the central stabilization move stated three ways.

2026-04-22 — Subject-determiner footnote narrowed from rejection to non-adoption. Earlier draft said "this paper rejects" CGEL Ch.16 §5; now says "this paper doesn't adopt the analysis... nothing in the argument hinges on the choice." Reason: Pullum and Payne both flagged the rejection as too brisk; the analysis is orthogonal to this paper's purposes.

2026-04-22 — §4.2 includes concrete before/after bracketing schemas (CGEL: NP → Det:{DP, NP[gen], PP} + Head:Nom; present: NP → Det:{NP, PP} + Head:Nom) with instantiations for *the book*, *Kim's book*, *about twenty books*. Reason: review board wanted the simplification claim "technically earned" rather than just stated.

2026-04-22 — §5 three-condition defectiveness test. A defective member remains inside a sub-category only if (i) paradigmatically integrated, (ii) contributes the same feature content, (iii) reduction is directional and local. Reason: principles the article handling rather than leaving it as a rescue operation; also used to defend *every* as a quantifier with one missing cell rather than an article-like residue member.

2026-04-22 — Predicative complement added to canonical NP function lists. Recognized as a canonical function alongside subject, object, complement-of-preposition. §7 notes the asymmetry (determinatives weaker on predicative complement) as a sub-cluster property. Reason: completeness of canonical-function statement; previously absent.

2026-04-22 — Terminology cleanup. "Categories not classes" for lexical-category referents (replace_all); "this paper/proposal/account" not "the present X"; "complement of preposition" not "PrepC" (which isn't a CGEL abbreviation; PC is predicative complement); "function as determiner" preferred to "fill Det function" where natural; en-dashes for fused-function labels (Det--Head not Det-Head). Reason: CGEL terminological hygiene + Brett's stylistic preferences.

2026-04-22 — Bringhurst pass: all em-dashes reduced to parens, semicolons, or commas. Zero em-dashes remain in the paper. Reason: typographic preference; em-dashes overused for parenthetical asides where parens or commas are cleaner.

2026-04-22 — New abstract opening (tricolon) and matched closing (chiasmus). Opening: "English determinatives have been treated as pronouns, as a category apart, and as heads of a functional projection; this paper argues that they are a fourth sub-category of Noun." Closing: "Whether fusion is retained or reanalyzed in any given construction, the categorial conclusion is the same: English determinatives are nouns." §1 opens with same tricolon (variant). Conclusion final: "Fusion may vary by construction; the categorial verdict does not: determinatives are nouns." Reason: external reviewer recommended the matched pair as the most rhetorically polished version.

2026-04-22 — Paper shipped twice today (8b9106a and 578cd8c). Local bibliography pushed to central via /push-bib (25 entries: Pullum & Miller, Bruening, Salzmann, Blümel & Holler, Chomsky UCLA, Hudson, Van Langendonck, Déchaine & Wiltschko, Anderson, Wiltschko, Van Eynde, Spinillo, Herslund, Maekawa, Pullum & Wilson, Reynolds 2013/2014/2021/2026 gender, Heine & Kuteva, Palmer 1924). LingBuzz submission MD created with copy/paste-ready fields. Well-named PDF: Reynolds-Determinatives_as_nouns_in_English-2026.pdf.

2026-04-24 — Thesis framing stabilized as asymmetric. Fusion is not an objection to nounhood, but the strongest positive case comes from reanalysing the simple independent determinatives as ordinary Head rather than obligatory fused Det--Head. Reason: reviewer convergence showed the stronger "categorial verdict independent of fusion" line was overclaiming and obscuring the real argument.

2026-04-24 — Membership criterion softened to the core external nominal functions. Subject, object, and complement-of-preposition are the clearest cases; predicative complement is treated as semantically licensed rather than required item-by-item across the sub-category. Reason: this resolves the mismatch between the criterion, the diagnostic table, and the later discussion of restricted determinative predicative-complement uses.

2026-04-24 — The determiner-slot subsection is now framed as a phrase-type consequence, not additional proof. "Independent NP use" replaces the lingering fused-head wording, and the payoff is explicitly that one noun-headed phrase-type disjunction disappears. Reason: align §4.2 with the preferred simple-case reanalysis and avoid suggesting that simple determinative heads still need fusion language.

2026-04-24 — The adjective comparison is now constructional, not lexicalized, and the internal-modification diagnostic is broadened beyond `few` with numeral cases (`the remaining three`, `the selected two`). Reason: stronger and fairer contrast with adjectival Mod--Head residues.

2026-04-24 — Lean appendix framing reduced to a machine-checked architectural sketch / audit trail. It is not presented as a proof that English determinatives are nouns. Reason: the current Lean file is a witness model of the architecture, not a data-linked formalization of the empirical argument.

2026-09-07 — **April-feedback integration into the September draft.** Restored the title *Determinatives as nouns in English*. September remains the base; the empirical analyses and their qualifications remain intact. Decisions below address both supplied reports together.

| Recommendation | Decision and reason |
|---|---|
| Explicit membership criterion | Adapted in §3: recurring constructions should share nominal projection with restrictions stated locally. This is a comparative grammatical criterion, not a necessary-and-sufficient test that proves the taxonomy by definition. |
| Three conditions for defective membership | Adapted in §7: paradigmatic integration, participation in characteristic contrasts, and locally restricted missing uses. Historical direction is not a necessary condition for synchronic membership. The article argument first establishes determinative membership; Noun membership still needs the category-level argument. |
| *Every/each* and *my/mine* | Adopted with distributional qualifications. *Every* shares universal quantification and singular-count selection with *each*, without sharing all its uses. *My/mine* supplies the closer restricted-noun precedent. |
| *Must/sake/dint* and the weakest-member aphorism | Rejected for this revision. The additional analogies repeat the point less directly than *my/mine*. The aphorism dismisses a legitimate boundary question rather than answering it. |
| Palmer and the historical genealogy | Adapted: Palmer 1924 appears through the passage reproduced in Reynolds 2013, explicitly identified as that source. No coinage claim or blanket claim that the modern split was a uniquely twentieth-century innovation: the inspected evidence does not establish either. Postal's and Sommerstein's underlying representations are distinguished from lexical taxonomy. |
| Expanded related work | Restored Bruening 2020, Anderson 1997, Déchaine & Wiltschko 2002 and C. Lyons 1999 in targeted prose. Bruening supports N-headed structure without proving D's lexical nounhood; Anderson's notional {N} is not this paper's inclusive Noun; the three pronominal projections concern structure as well as distribution. |
| Full April comparison table and remaining citations | Rejected the full table and automatic restoration of Herslund, Van Langendonck, Kripke and Spinillo 2000. The existing hierarchy table plus the added prose distinguishes the relevant analytical dimensions; Spinillo 2004 already supplies the developed rival. The additional names/naming and semantic surveys do not carry the English structural comparison and would expand its scope without resolving a present gap. This is a scope decision, not a negative verdict on those works. |
| Determiner-slot collapse | Adopted as new §8.1: Det {DP, NP, PP} becomes {NP, PP}. CGEL also licenses restricted plain-case NP determiners, so the original NP[gen]-only schema was incomplete. Features and PP determiners remain. |
| Three purported consequences of that collapse | Rejected as new deductions. Shared determining behaviour and the single-Det restriction are already functional generalizations in CGEL. Independent *some* and *Kim's* are already NPs; nounhood alone does not give them one internal structure, and genitive fusion remains. The phrase-type simplification and ordinary headedness are separate gains. |
| Three discriminators | Adapted into an explicit §4 preview and descriptive subsection labels: breadth of independent use, saturation and internal modification. Kept them as comparisons; saturation does not refute fusion, and adjectival fusion prevents external NP distribution from proving nounhood. |
| Pro-form gender | Adapted into §6 as a shared nominal pro-form generalization. It does not decide coordinate versus nested hierarchy, and non-nominal pro-forms prevent gender alone from delimiting Noun. Cited the actual 2025 unpublished manuscript: the old 2026 record pointed to an unrelated LingBuzz paper. No disputed numerical claims from that manuscript were imported. |
| Cardinals | Adopted as a paragraph on determinative, proper-noun and common-noun uses becoming alternations within Noun. Preserved constructional differences; no automatic fifth subcategory, extension to adjectival ordinals, or claim that complex numeral phrases are lexemes. |
| Grammaticalization | Adapted to one short paragraph grounded in the locally held C. Lyons chapter. History supplies context, not proof of synchronic category membership or of each present restriction. Additional Heine–Kuteva/Diessel citations are unnecessary for this limited claim. |
| Predicative-complement column | Rejected for the current fragment. It explicitly covers ordinary subject/object arguments. *They are few* requires a category/reading analysis before becoming a use-permission entry, and predication cannot be inferred from the existing argument column. Adding an unsettled column would weaken the controlled comparison. |
| Prototype/frequency claims, fragile clustering figures, and April's silent-head argument | Rejected. The matrix does not warrant stable prototype rankings or a robust 93% taxonomy result; annotated corpus frequencies are not capacities. The corrected silent-head discussion and the separation of saturation from anti-fusion claims remain. No April numbers were reinstated. |
| Failure conditions and conclusion | Adapted: repeated syntactic restrictions across an aligned pronoun–determinative inventory could favour Hudson; irreducibly different projection requirements in Det function could defeat the claimed economy over CGEL. These are comparative defeat conditions, not logical falsifiers of every possible coordinate grammar. Conclusion again states the fourth-subcategory argument directly. |
| Acknowledgement and capitalization | Kept Astra: the existing review records identify gpt-6-astra, and Brett had explicitly requested the acknowledgement. Removed the redundant “working draft for author review” sentence. Standardized Noun in Table 2. |
| Matrix instability and 155/232 mismatch | Kept detailed sensitivity results in the appendix because they directly qualify evidence used here; no separate paper is needed to make this argument responsible. Corrected this paper's local bibliography, whose old central note still said 232. Prepared `analysis/public-matrix-correction.txt`; no public resource has been changed, and the unauthenticated recovered 232-feature matrix must not silently replace the archived public file. |

**Projectibility diagnosis, kept separate from the edits.**

| Check | Status | Evidence and limit |
|---|---|---|
| Declaration | GREEN | §§1, 3 and 8 specify shared nominal projection in synchronic English, with lexical use restrictions. |
| Inquiry/use, warrant plan, revision rule | YELLOW | §8 fixes a matched descriptive comparison and names defeat conditions. This is retrospective grammar comparison, not a preregistered predictive study. |
| Non-trivial projection | YELLOW | Shared Det phrase structure supplies an explicit consequence, but the fragment reuses known judgments. No independent prediction about new constructions, acquisition or processing has been established. |
| Warrant versus world-side claims | GREEN | §5 distinguishes coded separation, corpus attestations and taxonomic inference; formal compatibility is not presented as empirical confirmation. |
| World-side order | GREEN | No inference from distributional stability to causal ordering, maintenance or control. |
| Stabilizer versus controller | GREEN | No homeostatic or corrective-control claim. |
| Level and mereology | GREEN | The rules describe linguistic constructions; the paper does not attribute agency or correction to a category. |
| Scope and field-relativity | GREEN | English is explicit in the title and §1; grammatical usefulness is not a claim of universal ontology. |
| Prospective revision | YELLOW | §§6–8 identify results that would favour rivals; they do not claim a prospectively validated decision procedure. |
| Positioning and conclusion | GREEN | §9 states the structural economy and distinguishes it from the further headedness proposal. |

The payoff is structural, but independent empirical projectibility remains unestablished. The evidence supports attested distributional patterns and a shared grammatical representation, not a maintained or controlled natural kind. The adopted edits make that architectural contribution explicit. A stronger predictive claim would require independent constructions and a comparison fixed before their evaluation; no such results were invented for this revision.

Validation: full XeLaTeX/Biber build, 19 pages; 23 resolved citation keys; all six trees and six tables retained; empirical section and replication appendix unchanged apart from the corrected matrix citation key. Selected changed pages and the bibliography were visually checked; no overfull boxes or unresolved citations/references remain. Existing font/header warnings persist. Automated style advisories were inspected without starting another prose pass.

## 2026-09-10 — Fourfold proposal and parsimony after the second author round

The matched fragment still fits nesting. Describing the extra level on determinative's inheritance path does not assert that Hudson requires an extra node in the total inventory.

The opening motivation follows the author's consolidation: primary-category and phrase-type reduction form one point, ordinary Head structure in the NP forms the second, and the unification of cardinal uses within primary Noun supplies a further benefit. All three Nom schemata permit a constructionally ordered postmodifier sequence. Shared-rule credit remains equal across ordinary-Head accounts; harmony is not counted again. ## 2026-09-12 Modifier series determine attachment

Brett identifies the unsupported peripheral treatment of *very* in dependent *the very few people*. The proposed analysis now assigns internal attachment to the *very/so/too/how* degree series on *few*, *many*, *much*, and *little* in dependent and independent uses alike. This supersedes the earlier rule choosing attachment solely by the presence of an external Det. The interpretation and head selection are constant across those uses; no second attachment is introduced for *very*.

Approximative and focusing adverbs have independently established NP-peripheral uses. Their broad determinative pattern is analysed through that grammar. Approximatives after an external Det with independent cardinals require internal attachment. Degree-modifying NPs such as *this* in *this much* and *a lot* in *a lot fewer* are also internal, following the same head-selection reasoning. All modifier permissions remain specific to the relevant head and construction.

CGEL p. 392, §7.10, (61), verifies internal *very* in *a very few mistakes* and peripheral *quite* in *quite a few mistakes*. CGEL’s completeness modifiers, including *marginally enough* and *absolutely all* (p. 432), qualify a universal claim about degree modification: the four-item generalization concerns the adjective-like degree series. The manuscript preserves this distinction in a short footnote.

The profile table now separates internal and peripheral modification. The argument weighs the localized degree series, grade, and comparative complementation together, with broad NP-peripheral modification contributing to the nominal connection. Shared NP rules earn equal credit under both ordinary-Head accounts. *Mine* remains ordinary Head; all trees and schemata are unchanged. The verified build is 9,594 words and 29 pages; see `notes/passes/2026-09-12-modifier-series.md`.

## 2026-09-14 — Correction: the manuscript-block counts were unsound

The evidence-type proportions reported earlier today are withdrawn for the 56 manuscript claims. They were computed over a convenience sample and presented as findings about the paper, including a "corpus worklist" of seven constructions said to have no attested claim. That inference does not hold.

The extraction that produced those 56 was asked for "approximately 80–110 NEW participation queries", a quota rather than a frame, and its own README describes the result as "a bounded selection from the full documents, not an exhaustive inventory". Proportions over an undefined sample estimate nothing. The decisive case: *the lucky few* appears eight times in the manuscript and is the worked example of Figure 6, the ordinary-Head versus fusion comparison for internal modification, and it has no record in the claim set at all. So the counts over internal modification were taken from a sample that omits the paper's central instance of it.

A further error compounded it: treating `evidence_type != retained_attestation` as "unsupported". A *CGEL* citation is evidence, and *the very few people* is uncontroversial. The label records provenance, not adequacy.

The 54 supplement claims are unaffected. They enumerate one table cell by cell, so proportions over them are meaningful: 22 attested, 32 not.

`coverage-2026-09-14.md` now states the frame and marks the manuscript-block figures as descriptive of the extraction only. What R1 asked for stands on its own authority, not on these counts.

## 2026-09-17 — Corpus screening pipeline built and run on *few*

Brett asked for a token-cheap route from the census worklist to corpus attestations. Built `tools/corpus-screen/` (query specs per construction, a browser gather recipe, `screen.py`) and the `/corpus-screen` skill on Haiku. First lexeme, *few* in independent argument use, NOW corpus: six queries, 466 lines saved to `corpus/independent_argument/`, screened by TypeSafe with the spec's yes/no question and a domain question; 441 accepted, 25 escalated, none rejected. Bare *few* (no determiner, computed exactly from the line) appears in 104 accepted lines, 70 of them with no visible antecedent or complement, the generic reading the manuscript illustrates with constructed examples (*Few would disagree*): for instance *few would dare underestimate*, *a luxury only few would get*, *but few have any idea* (dates and sources in the reports). These are candidates for a human reading, not attestations.

Three lessons recorded in the tool README and the routing profile. The script tool truncates its result at a few KB and the clipboard call hangs, so rows leave the page by rewriting the top document and reading it with the page-text tool. TypeSafe cannot be asked positional questions (it called *only a few were* bare), so `screen.py` computes left context and determiner exactly and the model gets semantic questions only. And a search string must be checked against what it returns: "few were" was 93 of 94 *a few were*, so the spec now targets bare *few* with strings the article cannot precede. The site rate-limits bursts; queries are spaced a minute apart.

## 2026-09-17 — Adjudication: opportunity-frequency reasoning from the independent-*whose* work (adopted)

Brett connected the independent-*every* result to his independent relative *whose* claim (`papers/seeds/independent-relative-whose-bayesian`, `papers/drafting/cgel-fragments/fragments/02-independent-relative-whose.tex`, and the passage in `papers/queue/grammaticality-as-kind`). There, absence is weak evidence because the licensing context is rare: independent relative *whose* (*a friend of whose had told us*) has low opportunity, so its near-absence leaves the prior diffuse and the construction marginal rather than excluded. Independent *every* is the mirror case: the opportunity is high, since independent *each* is common in the same corpora and every such context is one in which *every* could have appeared, and the fused-head use is absent across about 1,250 lines of COCA and NOW. High opportunity plus systematic absence is the strong negative posterior, the LBE pattern, not the *whose* pattern. The residue (ellipsis with a recoverable nominal, binomials) is a different construction and doesn't count against it. Status: **adopted**; the reasoning licenses keeping the exclusion while stating the residue, and it should be said in one sentence in the article without citing the seed, which is unpublished. The same reasoning classifies the other two results: article-plus-adjective compounds have high opportunity and attestation at volume (licensed, secondary use); *each of them were* is variable (gradient, stated with the rate). The *each*-versus-*every* independent counts that make the opportunity claim quantitative are one COCA List query (`each/every was/has/will`), not yet run.

## 2026-09-17 — The set-denoting unification elevated to §5.5 and the conclusion

Brett asked to elevate it. §5.5 gains a paragraph: compound determinatives and proper nouns share a secondary set-denoting use (article, pre-head adjective, plural); *CGEL* treats the proper-noun case as a secondary use within Noun (p. 521) and the compound case as conversion (p. 423, n. 43); within Noun one use covers both, and a separate primary D needs a conversion rule to reach the same facts. The conclusion carries one sentence to the same effect. This is the first place the paper claims a unification rather than economy, and it commits the argument to that shape: further unifications of the same kind are now what the case wants. ## 2026-09-17 — Unification argument completed in §5.5; genitive hedge replaced

Brett accepted the hunt's recommendation, correcting one item: Det is realized by NP or PP under D-noun (the paper says so in §5.2), not NP alone; the note is corrected and that candidate demoted. Applied: §5.5 adds cardinal numerals as the third case where *CGEL* invokes a secondary use to reach Noun, quoting p. 385 ("primarily determinatives but they have a secondary use in which they inflect for number and hence belong in the noun category"), with Reynolds (2026) as the independent case for both uses; a second paragraph re-presents the partitive transparency rule, degree modification by NP, peripheral modification, and the head genitive as rules *CGEL* states twice and the taxonomy once. §2.5 drops the hedge that compound genitives "may owe their genitives to the nominal component" and argues from *CGEL* §16.6 (p. 479): *everyone's* is listed among head genitives "marked by the inflection of the head noun", and the phrasal *everyone else's* and *somebody local's* put the suffix after the postmodifier, so the genitive is the NP's; the definition is stretched on *CGEL*'s categorization and not on D-noun's. The conclusion now counts three unified secondary uses. Two census claims amended (inf-018, inf-019). Build passes.

## 2026-09-17 — Open: *such* and exclamative *what*, adjective (this paper, following *CGEL* p. 435) or determinative (the 2021 matrix's coding)

Found while placing the articles in the 2021 clustering (`notes/2026-09-17-matrix-position.md`). The manuscript follows *CGEL* in calling *such* and exclamative *what* adjectives (§2.1, citing pp. 433–437); the 2021 matrix coded *such*, *what* and *whatever* as determinatives, and in that matrix *such* is *the*'s nearest neighbour, inside the dependent-only cluster with the articles, *every*, *no* and the *which* determinatives. The two works disagree, and the disagreement bears on the articles' coherence argument (the cluster that holds the articles contains the two items the paper calls adjectives) and on the typicality register (its only source for adjectives in predeterminer function is these two). Status: **deferred to Brett**; whichever way it goes, the paper should say so and note the change from 2021 if there is one.


## 2026-09-17 — Correction: no such/what disagreement between the two works

The entry above conflated two lexemes each. The 2021 matrix rows are determinative *such* (*such contract as may be executed*) and interrogative determinative *what* (`what_det`); the adjectives the manuscript calls adjectives after *CGEL* p. 435 are degree *such* and exclamative *what* (*such a disaster*, *what a disaster*). Brett caught it. The articles' cluster in the matrix is all determinative; the typicality register's source for adjectives in predeterminer function (the p. 435 pair) stands; nothing is deferred. `notes/2026-09-17-matrix-position.md` corrected.

## 2026-09-17 — Adopted: degree *such* and exclamative *what* are restricted determinatives

Brett adopted the reanalysis in `notes/2026-09-17-exclamative-such-what.md`. §3.1 gains a paragraph: *CGEL* classes the pair as adjectives because exclamative *what* "fails all of the determinative criteria (a)–(c)" (pp. 539–540); read as *CGEL* reads *a few* and *many a* (pp. 356, 394), the complex forms *such a* and *what a* meet the first two criteria and are as inert as *many a*, the partitive criterion fails for the articles too, the words are NP-initial with no other determiner in non-count and plural NPs, and no adjectival property remains; Van Eynde (2007, §5) independently finds *such a* and *what a* lexical rather than Big Mess. The pair are restricted determinatives, dependent-only like the articles, with *such a* and *what a* complex determinatives beside *many a*; kind *such* was already the determinative *such*. §2.1's footnote points to the reanalysis; the typicality register's adjective cell for predeterminer function is withdrawn (no adjective source remains); the census lexeme table now records *such* and *what* as determinatives by override with the §3.1 sentence as grounding. Builds pass; 1399 quotations resolve. The change from *CGEL* is stated in the text.

## 2026-09-17 — No complex determinatives: *a few* is external determination, *many a* is predeterminer modification

Brett's question ("are there really complex determinatives? Under the D-noun analysis does that hold up?") answered no, and adopted ("ok go"). Revises the same-day entry above: degree *such* and exclamative *what* are determinatives with two functions (Det with plural and non-count heads, predeterminer with an indefinite-article NP), not "restricted determinatives with complex forms". Grounds, all from *CGEL*: (1) the semantic hinge is positive vs negative paucal, not multal vs paucal (p. 391, [33]), and the contrast is "largely neutralised" under a definite determiner, where modifier *few* "has to be interpreted as greater than zero" and "an internal modifier never can" mark the clause negative (pp. 393–394, [65]), so the polarity tracks the Det slot, not a lexeme *a few*; (2) *CGEL* concedes *a few* "admits *very*, *good*, and *fair*" and calls the structure "sui generis" (p. 392), while its own argument (b) against a complex quantifier *a number of*, that a fixed phrase "doesn't provide a structure with potential for replacements and expansion" (pp. 351–352), applies to *few* unchanged (*the/those few*, *a good/fair/select few*); (3) *CGEL* already lists *such* among predeterminer modifiers and says a predeterminer "normally precedes a determiner" (p. 331), so *such a mess* has *a* in Det, and the same structure gives every inertness fact of *many a* (p. 394): adjacency, count-singular head, no fused use (a predeterminer isn't an NP, whereas *a few* and *a great many* have fused-head use, p. 394); (4) *CGEL*'s own principle that "essentially semantic" motivation doesn't establish a complex unit (p. 621, on complex prepositions, citing *a number of*).

Manuscript: §2.1 footnote, the modification paragraph on *a very few*/*quite a few*, the article-membership paragraphs (three new, with the *a few* vs *several* differences of p. 392 explained as indefinite-article-NP combinations and a NOW footnote for *a select few*), the §3.1 paragraph (six short paragraphs on the predeterminer analysis; Van Eynde kept; "no complex determinatives remain in this account"; cardinals over a hundred handed to the numerals paper), the fragment's *a few*/*many a* lines, and the §5.5 unification list. Stated residues: *several* takes definite determiners as a modifier but not *a*; the lower bound of three for *a few* is a convention of the phrase.

Apparatus: the COMPLEX-lexeme block written into normalize.py earlier today (making *a few*, *many a*, *such a*, *what a* lexemes) was the wrong direction and was reverted with the rejected retry patch; the phrase-head mapping (*a few* → *few*) was right. amendments.json may now restate a claim (expression, construction, status) or withdraw it, with the previous values kept; 18 census claims re-quoted, of which am-021 withdrawn (its sentence is gone), and *many a* claims re-pointed at *many*. The short cached Haiku batch (ind-075..qn-016) was deleted and re-called rather than patched around. lexeme-overrides.json and typicality.json notes updated.

Corpus awareness: **adopted** the numerals paper (`reynolds2026numerals`) for *CGEL*'s remaining complex determinatives, cardinals above a hundred, now cited in §3.1 for that boundary. The 2021 matrix rows *a_few*, *a_little*, *a_great_many*, *many_a* are phrases under this analysis; the manuscript's matrix paragraph doesn't list rows, so nothing changes there, but the matrix audit supplement should say so at its next revision (**deferred**).

Brett pointed to his 2024 *Journal of Linguistics* paper (`reynolds2024why`), which the manuscript didn't cite. Read in full. Adopted at three places: §2.4, where CGEL's adverb exception for *more* and *less* before adjectives is now named and the paper's contrasts with *much* and *little* cited, so the grade paradigm of the four holds across their uses; §5.3, for its survey of the determinatives that modify AdjPs, AdvPs, and PPs, their lexeme-by-lexeme idiosyncrasy, and the modified determinative phrases (*far fewer*, *much more*) that show the internal structure an NP headed by a determinative supplies; §2.7, for its scale-structure explanation of a distribution without a category difference, beside the Solt point, with Payne, Huddleston & Pullum 2010 on complementary distribution. ## 2026-09-18 — Second-round Elicit report applied; the economy claim restated on the three costs of a separate category

Brett's argument, in his words: "I1 gets you one less case of fusion than CGEL with no extra machinery. I2 gets you that but at the cost of a Nom headed by a D"; and "I2 also carries an extra main category which heavily duplicates much in the noun category (including pronouns)." Adopted as the line of §5.2 and §5.5, replacing "the D-noun categorization stated as a disjunction" and "left to do no work in the syntax", which the reviewer could fairly call question-begging. Fusion is held fixed for adjectives in all four implementations; CGEL uses it twice; the two ordinary-Head implementations remove the determinative use and share the saving; D-noun buys it by a reclassification, separate D by a disjunctive head condition plus a retained primary category whose defining profile duplicates Noun's, plus the conversion rule and the extended genitive definition. The reviewer's sensitivity recoding, reproduced in the audit, brings the two level only by pricing the disjunct at zero and not counting the category kept; the audit now says so and lists distinct conditions beside cell counts. The abstract's "shows" stays. Decision 2: unification with the input class stated (names, compound determinatives, cardinals; the articles, every, and the personal pronouns unattested). Decision 3: precedence rule and sensitivity table now; a human second coder for the disputed cells before submission (open). Also applied: M3 wording; Van Eynde's English split marked as extrapolation with his functor/marking treatment stated; the Lenchuk & Ahmed footnote answered on the paradigm; "held-out" replaced by "additional consequences"; 35 not 36 lexical cells; the response note's key check corrected to 44/51; the abstract's "dissolves" narrowed to the four CGEL forms; the secondary use recoded S in every account with one rule under D-noun and two under separate D.

## 2026-09-18 — Elicit parsimony-literature report: coextension reply adopted; audit redesign accepted and deferred

Brett ran the parsimony question (is X categories with X_s subcategories more parsimonious than X-1 with X_s+1?) through Elicit. Its negative claim, that raw category count is not a measure of grammatical simplicity, argues against a position the paper doesn't take; the audit counts rules, conditions, and lexical facts, and the conditions inventory already counts a construction-specific condition once (its "count S once" proposal). Its rival to D-noun, a separate D inheriting nominal projection from a cross-cutting superclass or feature, collapses: a feature carried by exactly the nouns and the determinatives is the superordinate node under another name, and a feature earns its place only by cross-cutting, which none does in the fragment. Decided (Brett: "1 yes, 2 yes, but not immediately"):

1. Adopted. One sentence in §5.5 after "the profile comparison is where the boundary is decided", the matching statement in the audit's results, and one method sentence in the audit naming the standard (complete representations, not category counts) with Rissanen 1978 as its only citation (verified through CrossRef: Automatica 14(5), 465–471; `rissanen1978mdl` in references-local.bib). MDL stays out of the main text. Elicit's proposed §5.5 sentence, which makes the economy conditional on the endocentric architecture, not taken: it concedes the feature alternative without the reply.
2. Deferred. Rebuild the audit as a declaration inventory: each cell lists the named declarations it uses and an implementation's cost is its count of unique declarations, so the reviewer's recoding becomes a computation. To be done before the human second coder (which changes what the coder codes) and before submission; about a day.

Also confirmed today, of the three second-round questions of 2026-09-18: question 1 (how strongly to claim separate D is D-noun under another name) is settled by the three-cost argument; question 2 is (a), unification with stated eligibility, as applied in §4.5; question 3 is (c) for now, precedence rule and sensitivity tables standing in, with a human second coder before submission. Brett: "1 is settled, 2 a. 3 c now".

Triage addendum: `reviews/external-2026-09-18/triage-round2.md` (gitignored).

## 2026-09-19 — Correcting *a few*: peripheral *a*, determining *few*

Brett corrected the 17 September analysis: “I see few as the Determiner with a as a peripheral modifier”, then instructed “fix it”. The earlier external-determination analysis was a misunderstanding. The companion correction note was reviewed in Roughdraft. In *a few mistakes*, *few* heads the NP in Det and *a* is peripheral to the outer NP headed by *mistakes*. Independent *a few* has peripheral *a* on the NP headed by *few*. The parallel cases *a little* and *a great many* receive the same analysis.

The singular-count condition belongs to *a* in Det function. Peripheral *a* has a restricted modifier permission, including the head-specific modifier conditions in *a great many* and the ordering of peripheral *quite* before *a*. Plural agreement of independent *a few* follows from *few*, without a new transparency claim. *The few mistakes* retains Det *the* and internal modifier *few*; *many a*, *such a*, and *what a* retain their predeterminer analyses.

The manuscript's modification discussion, article discussion, fragment, economy discussion, and conclusion are corrected, along with the corresponding coverage-audit notes and current census quotations. Original census and model outputs remain provenance. This supersedes the external-determination part of the 17 September decision, including its inference from genitive determiners and its claim that the combinations of *a few* follow from ordinary indefinite-article NP structure. The broader census refresh and declaration-based audit redesign remain separate work.


## 2026-09-19 — Register scope and the separate-D feature alternative

That formulation can share the head condition, head-genitive definition, and secondary-use schema; category change for D inputs remains explicit if CGEL's output categories are retained. The audit's existing four implementations and codes are unchanged. Its new compact comparison separates the feature alternative from the stated I2. This supersedes the 18 September claim of a strict cost advantage against every separate-D formulation: neither per-lexeme feature assignments nor wholly separate rules are necessary, and no common encoding has established that stronger ranking. The nominal profile remains the argument for giving the shared domain lexical-category status. Abstract, relevant body passages, conclusion, and supplement are aligned with that distinction.

2026-10-04 — Specialization within Noun, Brett's idea. If proper nouns specialize in realizing proper names, pronouns specialize in being pro-forms, though not all are (interrogative *who* isn't). Worked out with *CGEL* and inserted as two paragraphs at the end of the §2.4 Det discussion: each subcategory of Noun has a characteristic semantic specialization, each is disjunctive (pronouns: deictic or anaphoric, p. 425; determinatives: definiteness or quantification, p. 358), each leaves members outside it, and in both the pronoun and the determinative case the outliers are the interrogatives (*who*; *which*, *what*, *whichever*, *whatever*, p. 356). *CGEL* itself calls quantification "a semantic term" with "no one-to-one relation" to the determinative category (p. 358), naming *lot*, *number* and *numerous* among its other realizers, which is the §2.1 and §2.5 material. Det is kept apart as a syntactic function, not a semantic specialization (Brett: "remember that Det is a syntactic function, while pro-form is a semantic one"), and *CGEL* says Det "need not be realised by a determinative" (p. 358), so neither specialization marks a primary boundary. §6.2 gains a sentence: the semantic specializations split the same way as the grammatical connections (deixis with pronouns, quantification with *lot* and *number*), so they support coordinate rank. This replaces a sentence floated earlier the same day, which had the specializations differing between pronoun and determinative; with Det recognized as a function, both are semantic and the earlier premise no longer held.

Dropped *the pain and immobility became too much to bear* and *sympathy seemed too much to hope for* (and their footnote sources); kept *it seemed so little to give* as the one *little* example. 2026-10-15 — §5 (after the matched-heads discussion): *the some* added as a case where ordinary Head and fusion come apart (Brett: "accept all. Add the row"). Two paragraphs. (1) CGEL says dependent *some* has "just the determiner function" (p. 410), yet independent *some* takes *the* (*the some will become the many*; *one of the some who hope…*). Under fusion it would be a Mod–Head with no modifier use to license it, the anomaly CGEL records for *the both of us* (p. 377). (2) CGEL's condition predicts rarity but not occurrence. With *some* as Head nothing needs licensing, and the rarity is semantic: *some* is existential (p. 380), so after *the* it adds only that the set is part of a larger one. That's worth saying with a contrast, a partitive, or an earlier indefinite. This analysis is Claude's, not CGEL's. Footnote evidence: COCA 5 clear lines; iWeb 10 *the some who* against 53 *the some people*, none of the latter in a definite frame (15 *the* for *that*, 2 *same*, 24 stray *the*, 9 titles/garbled). Table `tab:matched-heads` gains a row: *the some who*, fusion "*some*: Mod–Head, with no modifier use" against ordinary "*some*: Head"; text count now six constructions. Argued as ordinary Head over fusion, not credited to the D-noun grouping. Record: `corpus/exclusion_tests/2026-10-15-brett-supplied-an-each-the-some.md`. Also checked: CGEL treats *some* "approximately" as an adverb (p. 385), so *the some 300 institutions* isn't a modifier use. *The all* left out because CGEL gives *all* a modifier use (*the all wool*).

2026-10-15 — Two additions (Brett: "make it so"). (1) §6.1: a new paragraph after the pronoun-input paragraph. 2026-10-15 — §6.1 third-person paragraph revised after a literature search (Brett: "get the pdfs we need and then revise"). Prior mention found: Halm 2021, lingbuzz/006012 (Table 1, p. 29), which gives *the*+adj+*me/you* 3SG verbal agreement but 1SG/2SG reflexives and possessives, with 3SG pronouns when two stages are contrasted (p. 30). The committed claim "the NP it heads is third person singular, as a common noun's is" held for the verb only. New text: the verb treats the NP as third person; reflexives and possessives keep the pronoun's person (*The 12-year-old me is kicking myself*) and switch to third person under contrast (Halm), as in our *someday the real you will wish he knew*. A new paragraph argues that the split favours the paper's secondary use over CGEL's conversion: a speaker-referring common noun can't bind *myself* (*\*Daddy is enjoying myself*, Halm p. 19), but *the 12-year-old me* can. Halm's data and table are cited, not his DP analysis (*the* lexically third person). Bib: `halm2021virtual` in `references-local.bib`, the manuscript we read. 2026-10-15 — Split sequencing and process (Brett: "proceed", after Codex's second opinion, `reviews/external-codex-venue-20261015/`). The structural paper is written first and goes to JL first. The taxonomic paper follows; Corela (December call) or JL afterwards is still open. Brett's process rules for the split:
1. Write the two new abstracts and conclusions first, before moving prose. Each states a contribution that doesn't require accepting the other paper.
2. Then allocate the existing sections, rebuild the transitions, and audit every cross-reference for a premise that has accidentally moved out.
3. Keep a single underlying evidence repository (this one: `corpus/`, `analysis/`), with each paper given its own guide to the relevant records.
4. Give each extended analysis one main home; repeat definitions and a few essential examples where self-containment requires them.
5. No "Part I / Part II" titles.

Codex's corrections are adopted for the structural paper: *the some who* defeats CGEL's stated licensing condition but doesn't by itself exclude conversion, a construction-specific account, or a revised fusion condition, so the paper must show why those repairs fail.

2026-10-15 — Structural paper titled *Fusion of functions revisited: Independent determinatives in English* (Brett, choosing from four options). The old working title made no sense. (2) A constituency test for the structural paper: ordinary Head groups *[hardly [anyone present]]* and fusion groups *[[hardly anyone] present]*, to be told apart with coordination or displacement diagnostics. (3) An inventory-wide test of the repairs: externally determined independent quantifiers that have no internal-modifier use (*the some who*, *the both of us*, …). If the pattern recurs, a one-off exception won't do. Designs to follow in `notes/split/`.

2026-10-15 — Det clause added (Brett: "yes, add it and proceed", after asking why *the some who* is a modifier-head case and not a determiner-head one). With *the* in Det, a fused analysis must make *some* a modifier-head, and for determinatives CGEL ties that to an internal-modifier use (pp. 410, 415), which *some* lacks. Added in the combined manuscript (§4.3) and in the structural paper's abstract and conclusion. "Ties" replaces "only": CGEL states the general principle (p. 410) and a sufficient condition for determinatives (p. 415), not an "only". The revised abstracts (merging the current and NBER versions after GPT pro and GLM-5.3) are now in both LaTeX drafts. The taxonomic draft keeps the opening question "Is *the* a noun?", with a comment saying how to drop it.

2026-10-15 — Test 3: *much* dropped as the lead case (Claude, on the COCA results; Brett to confirm). CGEL §7.11 (p. 393) restricts plain *much* to non-affirmative contexts, so *the much that remains* (COCA: 0, plus one literary hit licensed by a parallel with *the little*) is absent under either analysis and can't discriminate fusion from ordinary Head. The inventory argument rests on *some* and *both*, with *the all of* still to screen. The negative controls (*the any*, *the enough*) give no independent uses. Records: `corpus/exclusion_tests/2026-10-15-brett-supplied-the-little-much-any-enough.md`, `notes/split/2026-10-15-tests-2-and-3.md`.

2026-10-15 — Test 3 (the inventory of repairs) kept as a footnote or appendix check, not a section of the structural paper (Brett: "yes, keep it as a footnote or appendix check"). COCA showed no "exceptions become the rule" pattern: *much* confounded by polarity, *the much of* slips, *the both of* 99% personal-pronoun partitives (a fixed frame), *the all of* mostly a totality noun with two quantificational writers, controls empty. What it does show is that *the some who* isn't an artefact, and that conversions (*somes*, label *any*, nominal *enough*, totality *all*) look different from it. Record: `corpus/exclusion_tests/2026-10-15-brett-supplied-the-little-much-any-enough.md`.

2026-10-15 — Test 2 (the constituency test) dropped as an argument (Brett: "agree"). As designed it can't discriminate: CGEL's NP-peripheral scaling modifiers (§13, p. 437: *hardly a moment's hesitation*, *exactly that*) give fusion the ordinary-Head grouping *[hardly [anyone present]]* too, so the minimal pair parses alike under both analyses. What survives goes into the structural paper's compounds section: CGEL's own inventory supplies the adverbial premodifiers that Payne, Huddleston & Pullum (2007: 583) count as "a particular strength of the FF analysis", so that evidence is neutral. The combined manuscript's §4.5 sentence on differing groupings is to be qualified. Record: `notes/split/2026-10-15-tests-2-and-3.md`.

2026-10-15 — Priorities after the split (Brett: "make it so"): (1) the structural paper to JL, with full focus, starting from the allocation map; (2) the taxonomic paper next, venue chosen after the structural paper is out; (3) housekeeping in one batch (payload check and push, fusion-project remote, /push-bib at polish). 2026-10-15 — Structural paper reframed after a full reading of Payne, Huddleston & Pullum 2007 (notes in the structural repo). Thesis: fusion makes two departures from canonical structure, a category change on the head path (p. 571, "independently required" for hybrids) and a constituent with two mothers ("the major departure", p. 570); ordinary Head keeps the first and drops the second. *The some who* stays the discriminating case, argued as a dilemma (Brett: the no-counterpart clause "covers their asses but it's not a great theory"): PH&P's only no-counterpart case is the non-NP fused relative (p. 575), every determinative they analyse has a counterpart (p. 588), and their lexical distinction (p. 581) restricts rather than licenses; so fusion either lists *the some who* as an exception or turns its licence into the lexical list ordinary Head already states. Conceded: fusion derives the absence of pre-head adjectives with bare compounds from adjacency (p. 584), which ordinary Head states as a condition. *Once*/*twice*/*thrice* get a subsection; they fit the counterpart licence and don't distinguish the analyses. The draft body is now complete apart from the ellipsis literature, which a subagent is verifying (Brett's bibliographies hold none: no `.bbl` in the portfolio contains "ellipsis", and no central-bib title contains "ellips").

2026-10-15 — Structural paper: the ellipsis section became "Silent nouns", after a subagent's verified literature check (saved in the structural repo, `reviews/ellipsis-lit-check-20261015/`) pushed back that ellipsis (antecedent needed) and empty nouns (no antecedent; Panagiotidis 2003, Saab 2019) are different analyses. Pullum (1975: 175–176), read in the OSU scan, derives independent *all*, *any*, *some*, *a few*, *this*, *that*, *these* by deleting a head noun and uses the possessive (*\*all's satisfaction*, *\*some's tails*) as "a rather subtle test for 'nouniness'"; the paper concedes that a silent noun explains this gap better than fusion (whose case account wrongly excludes *everyone's*) or ordinary Head (which states it). The arguments that still apply to both silent-noun analyses: *the some who* needs an overtly excluded source; partitives have no head to restore (CGEL pp. 420–421); adjacency (PH&P p. 570 n. 5). Six verified entries added to the structural repo's `references-local.bib`; still to read through a library: Lobeck 1995 ch. 4, Günther 2018, Kester 1996 (JL), Llombart-Huesca 2002.

2026-10-16 — Structural paper: Pullum 1975 is cited like any other source; no remark on his being a CGEL author (Brett: "pullum requires no special handling"). Aside removed from §10 (fusion repo).

The gap is now presented as a fact every account states, falling on *all/some/many/few*; its semantic source is left open, since quantity and number are confounded in the data. §7 gains these as further counterexamples to CGEL's case-matching explanation. 2026-10-16 — Structural paper reframed on economy and unification (Brett: "yes, reframe on economy and unification"). The thesis no longer claims an empirical discriminator. §1 names the two places the analyses predict differently: which determinatives take *the* without a noun (CGEL's correspondence is hedged and has exceptions both ways, pp. 410, 415-416), and what precedes a bare determinative (adjacency favours fusion; *a lucky few* requires a statement in both). Basis: reviews/muse-wonder-second-mother-20261016/ (four models; synthesis) and corpus/premodification/. Decision owner: Brett. Drafting: Claude Opus 5.5.

2026-10-16 — Deferred (taxonomic paper): candidate for the open problem "an observation the separate-category rival fails to predict". Restrictive pre-head adjectives need a determiner with every noun subcategory that lacks one in its primary use: pronouns ("do not normally allow internal pre-head dependents", exception non-restrictive *lucky/poor/silly*, CGEL p. 430), proper names (*The young Isaac Newton* vs non-restrictive *Young Isaac Newton*, p. 521), determinatives (*the lucky few* vs \**Lucky few escaped*; corpus/premodification/). Under D-noun this is one condition; with D separate it is three statements, and fusion derives only the determinative case (adjacency). To develop: argue the unification is a prediction, not just economy; corpus-check bare restrictive adjectives with names and pronouns; check whether exclamative *Lucky few!* parallels *Lucky you!* (one fragment so far). Raised in the structural-paper session; owner Brett.

2026-10-16 — Considered and dropped (Brett: "more trouble than it's worth"): relabeling CGEL's Nom as NP (headed by N) and NP as a NomE above NP or DP. It would remove both of Payne et al.'s departures with D kept separate, and reduce Det–Head fusion to a double label on one edge. But discarding Nom loses the category-neutral layer that hosts attributive adjectives (*the lucky few*, *the idle rich*). It would also either move the {N,D} disjunct to modifier sites or split *the lucky few* from *the lucky survivors*, undo §9.3's Det consolidation, and break the structural paper's premise of CGEL categories unchanged.

2026-10-16 — The two papers are logically independent but evidentially entangled, and the dependence runs mostly from taxonomic to structural (discussed with Brett). (1) Structural paper: new §11 "If determinatives are nouns", conditional only. On that classification the {N,D} disjunct and the category change go, the pre-head adjective condition becomes one condition across pronouns (*the real me*; CGEL p. 430), proper names (*the young Isaac Newton*; pp. 520-521) and determinatives, and external determination becomes a secondary use. A non-restrictive residue stays with fusion. Nothing else in the paper assumes it. (2) Taxonomic paper: the abstract no longer says the classification holds under any internal structure. The profile evidence does; the secondary-use schema presupposes ordinary Head, since under fusion *a special someone* needs conversion. The paper cites the structural §11 as a payoff, not a premise. (3) The 16 Oct deferred lead for the taxonomic open problem is this schema's prediction applied to the structural paper's §6.1 data, not a separate observation. Owner Brett ("yes").

2026-10-16 — Terminology (Brett): a syntactic function is called a function, not a job, role, use, slot, or position. Dependent, independent, and secondary use stay, as CGEL's terms for kinds of use. Semantic and syntactic properties aren't listed as parallel items. Applied to both papers.

2026-10-16 — The structural paper uses no generative notions it rejects (c-command removed from §1; no movement metaphor). Rival mechanisms (null heads, silent nouns, Lobeck's strong agreement, Saab's selectional feature, Payne et al.'s list of rivals) stay only as descriptions of the analyses argued against.

2026-10-16 — Structural paper §3.1, second point, rewritten at Brett's request ("make it clear why simple heads is better or no worse"). CGEL's objection to its third approach is now stated as CGEL gives it (p. 421): with *nylon* as Head alone, *I prefer cotton shirts to nylon* gets the structure of *I prefer cotton to nylon*. The reply is no longer an argument from silence ("CGEL states no objection"). The objection needs a word with a Head-alone use for the fused use to be told apart from, and CGEL gives determinatives none (only *what*, *which*, *we*, *you* are also pronouns, p. 421), so by CGEL's own criterion ordinary Head loses nothing. CGEL's preference for "a unitary treatment" (p. 419, *several* against *the larger*) is answered by the phrase each word heads (a determinative's phrase is NP in every function; an adjective heads AdjP) and by the determiner range in the fourth point. *Nylon* and *double* stay fused as scope, per 2026-09-19, now with a pointer that §7's reply for *mine* (fused and pure head, p. 410) may extend to them. Pages verified against the CGEL PDF.

2026-10-16 — Structural paper: *nylon* and *double* no longer stay fused; this reverses the 2026-09-19 scope choice (decision owner Brett, "yes" to drafting option (b); drafted by Claude Opus 5.5, uncommitted for his review). CGEL's p. 421 objection to its third approach cites interpretation only ("understood as having modifier function"), unlike its objections to the pronoun and ellipsis analyses (pp. 419–421). Where form can be tested it follows the noun as Head, so the modifier function marks nothing of form (fusion has no understood noun in the syntax; the target is CGEL's stated restriction, bare or definite, which count nouns break both ways): *You take the cat shirt and I'll take the dog* is fine though *dog* belongs to none of CGEL's listed fused-modifier types (composition *nylon*, p. 417; *\*the sociology*, p. 415); bare *dog* is out and *a dog* fine (count), while bare *nylon* stands for one shirt and *?a nylon* degrades (non-count, p. 417, which CGEL puts down to indefiniteness). Judgments: Brett (*the dog*, *\*dog*, *a dog*, bare *nylon*); attested COCA lines *a kitchen sink rather than a bathroom* (The Farmer's Nest, 2012) and *a misdemeanor sentence rather than a felony* (Arizona Daily Star, 2019), gathered by hand by Brett. Plural *I'll take the dogs* judged questionable, so not used. *Double* treated as an adjective per Brett: CGEL's noun analysis rests only on *in doubles/triples* and it calls attributive *double* "probably" an adjective (p. 434). Fusion now remains only for adjectives and fused relatives, as the abstract already says. Loci changed: §3.1 (new example (ex:dog) and two paragraphs), §3.4, §7 title and closing paragraph, comparison table (tab:economy) row and caption, §9.2, conclusion. Footnote cites CGEL p. 217 (predicand) for keeping interpretation apart from function. The wider charge (CGEL letting interpretation settle function) is routed to `books/operating-manual-modern-english/notes/cgel-semantics-syntax.md`, not this paper.

2026-10-16 — Structural paper (ex:dog): two attested iWeb lines added for bare non-count *nylon* standing for one item (*a leather sheath rather than nylon*, budk.com; *a metal-bladed version rather than nylon, as the latter will probably snap*, growveg.com), beside Brett's constructed *I'll take nylon*. Lines pasted by Brett (iWeb `rather than nylon`, `instead of nylon/plastic/metal/wood/leather`); the pattern recurs across materials, recorded with the COCA batch in `corpus/exclusion_tests/2026-10-16-brett-supplied-bare-material-nouns.md`. 2026-10-17 — Structural paper: the analysis of *double* in *Mary earns double* is left open (Brett's choice among: fused adjective-head, plain AdjP in a non-object function, or open). §3.1 now says only that, as an adjective, it falls outside the nouns and nothing depends on its analysis; it's dropped from the ordinary-Head column's fused list in the comparison table. Corrects 13a2407, which had listed it as a fused adjective-head without that being decided (caught by a side agent).

2026-10-17 — Structural paper: *double* is a noun, as CGEL has it (pp. 418, 421, 434). This supersedes this morning's "left open". Brett's reasoning: before a definite determiner the predeterminer modifier is an NP (*half the cake*, *double that amount*), and AdjPs function as predeterminer only before *a* (Big Mess, *how large a piece*; CGEL pp. 331, 435; Van Eynde 2007). His COCA searches found no AdjP predeterminer before *the*, a demonstrative, or any genitive pronoun (record: `corpus/exclusion_tests/2026-10-17-brett-supplied-predeterminer-adjps.md`). One technical line, *400-fold the amount*, is set aside as marginal, and its category in that use can't be determined (CGEL p. 568 lists *three-fold* as adjective and adverb). *Mary earns double* now gets the treatment *nylon* gets, and fusion remains only for adjectives and fused relatives. The claim is limited to definite determiners because §8.2 itself has an AdjP predeterminer before *a*. Caution recorded: Brett (2024) holds that distribution alone doesn't settle category, so the paper rests *double*'s category on CGEL and uses the predeterminer facts as consistent evidence.

- line-319 contradiction fixed (*the some* needs *some*'s permission for external determination);
- introduction answers the "sunk cost" objection (fusion stays for relatives and adjectives, so the saving is in what each analysis licenses for determinatives);
- §6.2 covers singulative, past and term-of-office *once* (Payne et al. pp. 584, 592–593);
- (ex:dog) drops the *leather sheath* line, which has a material reading;
- a footnote states that interpretation can explain distribution but a syntactic function needs a formal correlate;
- §7 quotes CGEL's "pure heads" (p. 410).

Rejected, with reasons in TRIAGE.md: peripheral *a* as a duplicate, the rescue for compound genitives, hybridization as the comparator, *few* as a special fused head, and a parallel fusion fragment. Redundancy cuts S5 and S6 are on hold because they contain the trade-off statement.

2026-10-17 — Structural paper: claim-by-claim audit of Payne, Huddleston & Pullum (2007) (Claude subagent; run `reviews/payne-audit-20261017/` in the fusion repo, gitignored) applied in full at Brett's instruction ("We do what's honest, clear, and best supported").
- **The cost of fusion is restated.** Fusion's cost is now "a constituent with two functions", not "two parents" everywhere. Payne et al. say the category "will in effect have two mothers" but that "in the simple case the two mothers happen to coincide" (pp. 570–571). Their trees give Det–Head two parents (NP, Nom; pp. 582, 588) and Mod–Head one Nom that the constituent both modifies and heads ((19d), p. 588), unless a post-head dependent splits the functions ((24), p. 590). The intro's argument about relations over a node's parent is split by case.
- **Figure fig:few is redrawn on (19d).** It had given *few* two parents. The §9.2 fragment's Mod–Head schema is corrected to Mod(n,x), Head(n,x).
- **Gaps answered:**
  - footnote 43 (p. 598): the only comparison they make, in passing;
  - the claim that morphology mirrors fusion (pp. 565, 587–588; n. 30);
  - independent PP determiners like *up to twice* (p. 589), as PPs with NP-like distribution (n. 7, p. 572);
  - the mechanism for singulative and past *once* (p. 592);
  - category from the modifiers (p. 583);
  - their own statement that *some* etc. "never permit a preceding determiner" (p. 588), now cited at *the some who*.
- **Fixes:**
  - *more than once* (head *more*, p. 589);
  - n. 5 on p. 571, not 570;
  - pp. 589, 586–587, 570–571 citations corrected;
  - term-of-office *once* heads an NP (consistent with §3.1);
  - footnote on the referee's judgment of *this last once* (p. 586 n. 26).
- **Redundancy pass.** P2 withdrawn (its target paragraph now carries the parentage facts). S5 and S6 released.

2026-10-17 — Structural paper: new architecture adopted. Brett accepted muse's plan as the base with all of Claude's amendments (run `reviews/structure-muse-20261017-115410/` in the fusion repo, TRIAGE.md). Decision owner: Brett; assisting: muse (Meta, structure plan) and Claude (triage). The paper is organised by function, following Payne et al.'s inventory of the dependents fusion involves (2007: 571: determiner, modifier, and in fused relatives subject or prenucleus): Det–Head and Mod–Head go whatever the category; Subject and Prenucleus stay. Case is the diagnostic the two halves share (CGEL p. 420 by analogy with pp. 1073–1074), graded where it shows, so subject relativisation and the infinitival ban carry the categorical weight. The *nylon*/*dog* test moves to §2 with the criterion. The rivals' premises and conditional framing go in §2, with the new silent-noun point at *the some who*. The cost table comes before fused relatives, which come last. The fused-relatives section engages the free-relative literature. The opening is rewritten by function around CGEL's p. 1055 pair. Still open: the non-NP fused relatives (Payne et al. p. 575), a new subtitle, and whether any short rivals section survives. Sections: 1 intro (800), 2 analyses and criterion (1,500), 3 Det–Head (1,500), 4 Mod–Head (2,000), 5 *once*/*twice*/*thrice* (800), 6 what each analysis states (600), 7 fused relatives (1,800), 8 conclusion (400).

2026-10-17 — Structural paper: fused-relatives section drafted by Claude in the fusion repo at `drafts/fused-relatives.tex`, not yet \input; open items are in `drafts/NEEDS-FROM-BRETT.md`. - Inseparability is omitted.
- Literature on frameworks internal to generative grammar is kept out at Brett's instruction.

2026-10-17 — Structural paper, §3 case paragraphs rewritten after Brett asked whether §3 needed another round (decision: no second muse round, one targeted fix, then §4). New argument: the genitive divides the fused determiner-heads (*either's*, *neither's*, *each's*, *everyone's* and *somebody's*, CGEL p. 479 head genitives, against CGEL's "completely excluded" four, p. 420). Each analysis needs a list (fusion its exemptions, ordinary Head its gaps, like the reflexives, p. 426). The criterion asks whether the form varies with a function, not which list is shorter. In §3 the functions are constant across the division, so the form follows the word. In §7 the word and its main-clause function are constant, and the form follows the function inside the relative clause. CGEL's p. 420 argument is acknowledged as aimed at the pronoun analysis but bearing on ordinary Head too. §7's p. 420 analogy paragraph realigned. Pages 420, 426 and 479 checked against the CGEL PDF.
