2026-10-17 — Structural paper §4 (Mod–Head) drafted by Claude (Opus 5.5) at `drafts/mod-head.tex` in the fusion repo, after Brett said "proceed with section 4" (about 1,470 words of main text, 1,840 with footnotes; budget 2,000). Mod–Head goes for adjectives as well as determinatives, as the accepted architecture says ("whatever the category"). This replaces the old draft's position that fusion stays for adjectives (decision of 2026-10-17 on *double*: "fusion remains only for adjectives and fused relatives"). Argument: both analyses put an AdjP or DP at the head of the nominal (Payne et al. p. 569) and keep the category (CGEL p. 418). Nothing in form shows the modifier function. (1) The correspondence with the modifier use (CGEL p. 415) fails both ways: *the some who*, *the both of us* (p. 377), and *every* and *various* (p. 416). (2) The determiners follow the word: *the lucky few* takes a noun's range, the *rich* type only *the* (p. 417). (3) Which adjectives head a nominal is a list: *the main* is out though *main* is attributive-only (pp. 416, 555, 558), and CGEL concedes the licence "must in the last resort be stated in syntactic terms" (p. 416). (4) Ordinary Head meets CGEL's unitary-treatment demand (p. 419). (5) The genitive divides the fused modifier-heads by word: *latter*/*former* freely, *poor* in edited prose, *rich* web only, *few* almost never; counts are from capped displays. (6) *A lucky few* costs both analyses a statement; fusion can't make *few* a bare Mod–Head without licensing \**Lucky few escaped*. Also: §4 keeps the *a few* decomposition short (peripheral *a*, CGEL p. 392 order facts and expansions), and polarity and *many a* go in a footnote. Corpus-awareness: the taxonomic sibling's deferred point on restrictive pre-head adjectives (entry of 2026-10-16) is **deferred** here too, since §4 states the condition for determinatives only. Muse review running in `reviews/muse-sec4-20261017-180414/`. Constructed examples are flagged for Brett (K1).

2026-10-17 — Structural paper §2: the muse review (`reviews/muse-sec2-20261017-183900/`, TRIAGE.md) is applied in one pass. (1) The criterion is stated as a burden-of-proof rule grounded in Payne et al.'s "departure from canonical structure": no second function unless some property of form varies with that function and not with the word alone, which is the test §§3–4 apply. (2) The *nylon* demonstration is rebuilt. CGEL's own pair keeps word and form constant, CGEL's determiner restriction fails on (ex:dog), and the countability facts are conceded to be predicted by both analyses. The conclusion is "not licensed on this criterion". (3) Ordinary Head is defined generally (DP, AdjP; external determiner; nominal-internal complements, CGEL pp. 411–412; permissions). (4) Figure 1b is credited to Payne et al.'s line convention (p. 570 n. 4), and the hybrid tree's lack of a Nom is acknowledged. (5) Case gets a preview pair. (6) The hybrid cost claim is narrowed to kind and extent. (7) The rivals' taxonomy moves to a footnote, with Payne et al.'s n. 2 quoted in full. (8) Ultimate head is defined, the marks are glossed, and the data note says who labelled what. Main text is about 1,560 words against 1,500. Drafted by Claude (Opus 5.5); Brett to read.

2026-10-17 — Structural paper: ordinary Head is version A, CGEL's tree with the dependent function removed (the DP or AdjP functions as head of the nominal and nothing else). The old draft's version B, in which a determinative builds an NP around itself in every function (Det:{NP,PP}), is dropped from the structural paper. Decision: Brett, after Claude (Opus 5.5) set out the two versions and recommended A. Reasons: §§2–4 are written on A; B gives determinatives a noun's phrase structure, which is the taxonomic paper's structure (the older draft `main-anon.tex` states Det:{NP,PP} and "What was analyzed as a DP is an NP ultimately headed by a determinative-noun") without that paper's argument; and §2's claim that ordinary Head changes category only where fusion does is true of A and false of B. Consequences: §6 needs a new condition of failure in place of the structure plan's "shared projection" condition, namely the criterion run in reverse (ordinary Head is wrong for a word if, standing alone, it shows some property of form that varies with the determiner function and not with the word); the old section "If determinatives are nouns" becomes a pointer in §8. Corpus-awareness adjudication: the NP projection is **deferred** to the taxonomic paper. The taxonomic paper's dependency on this one (a determinative can be an ordinary head under a determiner, as in *a special someone*) is met by A. Its outline's instruction to cite the structural paper's §11 "as a payoff" must be checked against whatever §8 ends up saying.

2026-10-17 — Structural paper §7, paragraph on PP, AdjP and AdvP fused relatives: the sentence saying the head analysis "needs a new kind of modifier for three categories" is replaced, at Brett's request ("yes"), after he observed that in Payne et al.'s tree (8) the relative clause is a modifier inside a PP. Both analyses have a relative clause as modifier inside these phrases. The paragraph now says that fusion keeps the four types as one construction, in which the head of the phrase is also the clause's prenucleus, while the head analysis splits them: the NP type is an ordinary head with a relative clause in its nominal, and the other three need a relative clause modifying a non-nominal head, although Payne et al. call the four types similar (p. 575). Checked alongside: CGEL classes *everywhere*, *somewhere*, *anywhere* and *nowhere* as compound determinatives heading NPs (p. 423), so *everywhere you go* isn't a counterexample to "standard relative clauses are modifiers only within Nom".

2026-10-17 — Structural paper §6 drafted (`drafts/costs.tex` in the fusion repo, label sec:costs) after Brett said "proceed with section 6". About 430 words and one table, against a budget of 600. It is built on version A of ordinary Head, so the old §9's material on version B is dropped: the rows on phrase category and modifier domains, Det:{NP,PP}, the degree-modifier subsection and the shared-projection condition of failure. The table separates what both analyses state as lists of the same kind (determinatives standing alone, *none*/*mine*, external determination, the determiners taken, adjectives heading a nominal, the genitive, *a lucky few*, change of category) from the three differences (one structure for *few* against two; the pre-head adjective derived or stated; constituents with two functions). The trade is stated as in §3's closing, not as the structure plan's "ordinary Head states strictly less", which the body doesn't support. The new condition of failure is the criterion run in reverse, in case, form and order. Pending: a row for *once* after §5, and a supplement rebuilt on version A. Drafted by Claude (Opus 5.5); muse review running in `reviews/muse-sec6-20261017-221323/`.

2026-10-17 — Structural paper §6: the muse review (`reviews/muse-sec6-20261017-221323/`, TRIAGE.md) is applied in one pass. (1) The condition of failure no longer sets a threshold of "a second such gap". It states the test so that it can be run (one word, with and without the function, differing in form as §2 defines form). It concedes that the pre-head adjective passes the test and that fusion derives it, and it states the weighing as a judgment, resting on *a lucky few*. (2) "Ordinary Head never combines them" is narrowed to constituents, with bare *whichever he gave me* acknowledged (the two departures meet in one NP at different nodes). (3) The double count is removed: the row for *few*'s structures is merged into the second-function row, and "one structure" is argued by CGEL's own standard of generality (p. 419). (4) CGEL's following-modifier condition (p. 416) is a new row. (5) The table cells are made exact (determinatives and adjectives separated; the genitive cells state each analysis's expectation and list; the compounds added). Not accepted: moving the external-determination default below the line, since it is a default with exceptions in both directions on either analysis. (6) The adjacency derivation and its *a lucky few* exception now share a row. (7) Readability fixes. About 500 words plus the table. Raised for Brett, not applied: the old draft's argument that pronouns and names need the same adjective condition (NEEDS-FROM-BRETT M1). Drafted and triaged by Claude (Opus 5.5).

2026-10-17 — Structural paper §6, correction to the triage entry above: the closing paragraphs no longer say the pre-head adjective "passes" the criterion's test and then weigh it, which contradicted §2's rule and §3. They now say it "comes nearest to passing". It fails because the adjective is fine in *a lucky few*, where CGEL has the fused determiner-head *a few* (p. 392), so the gap follows the determiner function only if fusion reanalyses *a few* at the cost of a statement. A property that did pass "would license the second function". The test for the modifier function is §4's (a form found only with the function, or an order fact only a modifier relation predicts). Caught in a consistency check before Brett's read.

2026-10-18 — Structural paper §4: the pronoun-and-name argument is accounted for in the text, not left out. Brett: the M1 question read as "this fact was in before because it looked like it was on our side but now that it doesn't, I've dropped it"; "we need a proper accounting, and we need to show the cards whichever way they lie." Six paragraphs follow the *a lucky few* paragraphs in `drafts/mod-head.tex`, and §6's table row, trade paragraph and last paragraph point to them. They take three questions from CGEL. (1) Pre-head dependents with no determiner: CGEL states the exclusion for pronouns (p. 430) and derives it from fusion only for the compounds (p. 423), so ordinary Head's condition extends one the grammar has to a second category; Matthews (2014: 10) treats *tall someone the same way. (2) Non-restrictive adjectives with no determiner (*poor Henry*, *Lucky you!*; pp. 520–521, 430): bare determinatives seem to take none, which both analyses predict, since CGEL limits the exception to particular adjectives with the core personal pronouns. (3) Restrictive adjectives with a determiner: the old draft's *the real me* and *the young Isaac Newton* don't help ordinary Head under CGEL's categories, because a pronoun with a determiner is a common noun (p. 429 and n. 46; *the real you is* 10, *are* 0), and names select determiners "in the ordinary way" in secondary uses (pp. 520–521); the determiner comes from the singular count use and doesn't extend to plural *few*. Verdict: the derivation saves a category in a condition, not the condition. CGEL and Payne et al. (p. 584) pages checked against the PDFs; Matthews pp. 10, 28–29, 102 against the text conversion. Pending: Brett's judgments on four constructed examples and a COCA hand search for *Poor everybody!* (fusion repo NEEDS-FROM-BRETT M2, M3). Not yet muse-reviewed.

2026-10-18 — Corpus awareness, deliberately distinguished: the old draft used *the real me* as a pronoun taking a restrictive adjective after a determiner, and the taxonomic paper's §6.1 uses *the real you* (third person singular agreement) for its noun schema. The structural paper keeps CGEL's categories fixed, so it treats *the real me* as a common noun by conversion (CGEL p. 429 n. 46). The taxonomic paper's use stands on its own premises and is untouched.

2026-10-18 — Matthews (2014), *The Positions of Adjectives in English*, read for both papers at Brett's request (fusion repo `reviews/matthews-read-20261018/`: brief, four subagent reports saved verbatim, manifest with hashes; chapter 5 read by the parent session). Adopted in the structural paper: p. 10 (*tall someone* as "a matter of 'position' only") and p. 102 (*precious little*, a footnote on *precious few*). Raised with Brett, not applied: pp. 28–29 (*a partly cotton shirt*, no *a partly cotton*) as a test of §2's *nylon* argument (NEEDS-FROM-BRETT M4). Deferred to the taxonomic paper: ch. 5 §§5.13–5.14 (pp. 84–85; the determinative category as defined by the determining function alone, "at best factitious"), pp. 174–178 (categories justified "if, and only if" a general statement applies to their members; doubts that they form a hierarchy; CGEL's nine categories as a framework artefact), pp. 16–32 (what exhaustive word classes are for; the medieval *nomen* with substantive and adjective subclasses). The taxonomic paper doesn't cite Matthews yet.

2026-10-18 — Correction to the "deliberately distinguished" entry above. The first draft of §4's third question said *the real me* is a common noun by conversion (CGEL p. 429 n. 46). That contradicted the taxonomic paper, which argues from Halm's reflexive data for a secondary use over conversion (`determinatives-as-nouns.tex` ll. 838–840: *The 12-year-old me is kicking myself* against *Daddy is enjoying myself*). §4 now gives both views, shows the reflexive fact, and rests its point only on what both views share: the head is singular and count (*the real you is*), so it needs a determiner whatever modifies it. Adjudication: adopted in part (the taxonomic paper's reflexive evidence is cited, via `halm2021virtual`, which was copied into the fusion repo's references-local.bib); the classification question is left open in the structural paper. The plural-name case the argument depends on (*Young Kennedys still go into politics*) is put to Brett with both consequences (NEEDS-FROM-BRETT M2, item 4).

2026-10-18 — Structural paper §4, M2 answered (Brett: "m2 fine"). The constructed examples stand as marked: *Lucky few!* bad, *Remaining few were the hangers-on* bad, *Lucky survivors were airlifted out* good, *Young Kennedys still go into politics* good, *Volunteers were precious few* good. So the countability explanation in §4's third question stands: a plural name takes a bare restrictive adjective, and the determiner with *the young Isaac Newton* comes from the singular count use.

2026-10-18 — Structural paper §4, M3 result. Brett's COCA search `poor|lucky|silly everybody|everyone|somebody|someone` gave 28 lines, two in the use: *Poor Val. Poor everybody.* (*New Yorker*, 2005, fiction) and *Lucky you. - Lucky everyone.* (*Everwood*, 2004, TV). Record: `corpus/exclusion_tests/2026-10-18-brett-supplied-adj-compound-exclamatives.md`; raw lines private in the fusion repo (`reviews/corpus-poor-everybody-20261018/`). Consequence: §4's second question now favours ordinary Head. Ordinary Head adds them to CGEL's lexical exception (p. 430). §3's "Bare determinatives take no pre-head adjective" now carries "apart from a few exclamations such as *Poor everybody!*", and §6's table row and last paragraph say so. The draft treats both lines as acceptable; Brett passed the lines without a judgment and has been asked to say if either is marginal.

2026-10-18 — Structural paper §2, M4 answered in part (Brett: "I prefer pure cotton shirts to partly nylon is good"). The fused use admits *partly*. M4 as posed said that this would license Mod–Head fusion for *nylon*. That skipped half the criterion's comparison: whether *partly* also occurs where *nylon* has no modifier function. Matthews's two negatives don't supply it, since *these largely glasses* is the count sense and *a partly cotton* fails on *a* (non-count *nylon* resists *a*, CGEL p. 417). Put to Brett with both consequences stated in advance (NEEDS-FROM-BRETT M5): if *I prefer cotton to partly nylon* or *This shirt is partly nylon* is good, *partly* goes with the composition meaning and not with the modifier function, and §2 stands on a test that was actually run. If both are bad, the criterion licenses Mod–Head for *nylon*, which touches the thesis sentence and §6's "none outside fused relatives", not only §2. `analyses.tex` is not touched until the answer is in.

2026-10-18 — Structural paper §2, M5 answered (Brett: "those two seem good to me" for *I prefer cotton to partly nylon* and *This shirt is partly nylon* / *The frame is largely steel*). *Partly* occurs where *nylon* has no modifier function, so it goes with the meaning "made partly of", not with the modifier function. §2's claim that nothing in *I prefer cotton shirts to nylon* varies with the modifier function stands. A footnote in `drafts/analyses.tex` now records the test, citing Matthews (2014: 28–29) for the modifier use. Adjudication with Matthews: adopted for the data; his suggestion that the modifier use is an adjective by conversion is reported, not adopted.

2026-10-18 — Structural paper §2, the *partly* footnote: the predicative example is now the attested *This lava is partly magma* (conserve-energy-future.com, supplied by Brett; the page refused an automated fetch, so the quote rests on his paste), in place of constructed *This shirt is partly nylon*. The footnote's conclusion is narrowed from "goes with the meaning 'made partly of'" to "isn't tied to the modifier function", since *partly* also modifies predicates that aren't about composition (*It's partly a question of money*).

2026-10-18 — Structural paper §§3, 4, 6: muse review of the pronoun-and-name accounting applied (fusion repo `reviews/muse-sec4-6-20261018-065227/`, TRIAGE.md). Brett: "run the muse review on §4 and §6". Seven findings accepted, one not applicable (a misread: the draft has no *Paris* illustration). The verdicts are softened. Question 1: the grammar already states a ban of this kind for pronouns (CGEL p. 430), but ordinary Head's condition isn't that statement, since it has to mention a determiner and *a*; fusion's derivation saves that statement, not a kind of statement. This replaces "a second category in a condition the grammar has". Question 2: two *every*-compounds are attested in the pronouns' exclamation, each after the same exclamation with a name or pronoun. Fusion's exits are now conversion, a listed exception, or an NP-external adjective (which separates *Poor everybody* from *Lucky you!*). Verdict: a listed exception either way, against a principle only on fusion, "though not by much". Question 3 is unchanged in substance: the reflexive moves to a footnote with Halm's contrast case, and CGEL's common-noun reading is stated as a presumption. Corrections to this log's earlier entries today: Matthews p. 10 is no longer adopted (cut as an overreading, since his position-vs-function remark is tentative and about something else); "the compounds with a personal base share the pronouns' exception" is withdrawn; §6 no longer argues that the gap "behaves like the pronouns' gap". In §6 the compounds now loosen the gap's tie to the determiner function, as *a lucky few* does, and the pronoun point is a cost point only. Payne et al.'s p. 584 remark is now marked as said of a silent-head analysis, in §3 and §4.

2026-10-18 — Aarts on empty heads, checked against what's public (Brett: "is my proposal equivalent to Aarts's 2024 empty heads proposal?"; "why only the abstract"). The 15 Oct literature check stopped at the ISTAL 26 abstract page and didn't look for Aarts's other statements. Found today: (1) Aarts's Substack post "What the hell is a determiner?" (14 Dec 2024). It sets out three analyses of *these* in *I really like these* (pronoun; a zero Head, with *these* "a determinative functioning as a Determiner"; CGEL's Determiner-Head) and defers fused functions to a later post. (2) Wikipedia's *English adjectives* page attributes an empty-head analysis of *the very poor* to *Syntactic Gradience* (2007), pp. 129–136. The local `aarts2007` is a 43-page excerpt without those pages, and it describes CGEL's fused heads neutrally as one end of a gradient of "mixed" structures. (3) Wu, Zhen (2021), *Exocentric Noun Phrases in English*, UCL PhD thesis supervised by Aarts (eprint 10118908), on *the rich* and fused relatives, arguing against a unified account; bot-walled, not read. (4) Aarts (2004), "Grammatici certant", *JL* 40(2): 365–382 (DOI 10.1017/S0022226704002555), was read in full (UCL Discovery open copy). It doesn't discuss fused heads or *the poor*, and its "fusion grammar" means mixing frameworks, so it's a false lead. Answer to Brett: not equivalent. Aarts keeps the determinative as Determiner and adds an unpronounced head; ordinary Head makes the DP or AdjP the head and adds nothing silent (§2's third commitment). His empty heads also cover fused relatives (*what I saw*), where the structural paper keeps fusion. The two agree only in rejecting a constituent with two functions in Det–Head and Mod–Head. §2's present classification of Aarts (2024) with the silent-noun analyses is accurate. Pending: read *Syntactic Gradience* pp. 129–136 and Wu (2021). Then §2's footnote should cite a published or public statement (the 2007 pages if they bear it out, and the Substack post), not only the abstract, and say in a sentence what the accounts share and where they part.

2026-10-18 — Ordinary Head against an empty-head analysis (Aarts), predictions compared at Brett's request ("are they functionally equivalent? Do they make different predictions?"). All of this is conditional on the one thing Aarts's public statements commit to: the determinative or adjective keeps its dependent function and position (Substack, 14 Dec 2024). Whether his empty head is anaphoric (features from an antecedent) or a fixed zero with its own content is open. On surface strings, with each analysis's stipulations in place, the two are equivalent. On what each structure predicts, they aren't. (1) The empty-head analysis sides with fusion on the order facts: it derives *\*Lucky few escaped*, and so inherits *a lucky few*, and *Poor everybody* if the compounds are Det plus empty head. §4's three-question accounting applies to it as to fusion. (2) It makes fusion's correlation between the modifier use and heading after a determiner structural, so *the some who* against no *the some people*, *the both of us*, and *her every move* against \**her every* go against it in both directions; ordinary Head lists the cases. (3) Determiners follow the overt word, not the head (§2, ex:dog: bare *nylon* and \**I'll take dog* with the same antecedent *shirt*). That fits ordinary Head and fails under either reading of the empty head, unless bare *nylon* is reanalysed as the material noun, which concedes ordinary Head for that case. Aarts's own examples don't include this type. (4) In fused relatives, which Aarts covers, case follows the relative-internal function (49% against 14%), which an empty head allows, but *whomever* as internal subject after a preposition (14%) against *whom* as subject in ordinary relatives (390 against 449,450) shows the matrix function matters too, which only fusion gets. The comparison isn't like for like, and attraction to the adjacent preposition is an alternative. Notational between them: the interpretation and number of *the rich*, the *the*-only restriction, the genitive division. Status: deliberately distinguished. Offered, not applied: a sentence in §2's determiner paragraph saying that a silent head wouldn't predict the countability facts.

2026-10-18 — Structural paper §2: the CGEL p. 217 illustration of interpretation against syntax is replaced. Brett: "this is misleading". The draft quoted "semantically predicated of Ed, but Ed is not syntactically the subject" for *Ed was considered quite competent*. CGEL's sentence ends "the subject of *quite competent*", and in that passive Ed is the subject of the clause, so the truncated quotation said something false. Now: in *She considered Ed quite competent* the AdjP is predicated of *Ed*, which is the object of *considered*, and CGEL calls *Ed* the AdjP's predicand, not its subject (p. 217, rechecked against the PDF). The old main file (`fusion-of-functions-revisited.tex` l. 148) has the same truncation; it is superseded by the drafts and will be replaced when they are assembled.

2026-10-18 — Same fix in the old main file (`fusion-of-functions-revisited.tex` l. 148, footnote), at Brett's request. Both versions now gloss *predicand* as a semantic relation and *subject* as a syntactic one, citing CGEL pp. 217 and 252 ("This semantic predicate applies to the predicand, generally subject or object", p. 252, checked against the PDF). Brett asked whether predicand is semantic: yes, on CGEL's definition. Which syntactic functions can serve as predicand is grammatically restricted (an indirect object can't be the predicand of a predicative adjunct, p. 250), but the relation itself is semantic.

2026-10-18 — Structural paper §5 drafted (`drafts/once.tex`, label sec:once) after Brett said "draft the next section". About 900 words, against 800 (900 at most). Built from Payne et al. (2007) pp. 584–597, read in full from the PDF, and the structure review's plan. Version A throughout; the old draft's subsection (version B: *once* heading an NP that functions as modifier in *the once mayor*) not carried over. Order: the five uses (sequential *once* a preposition for them, so no fusion); the category argument, accepted; then the claimed gains, each answered from §§3–4: morphology (simple *some*, *few* fused without compounding; their n. 30), with and without a determiner (the default from the modifier use, §4), the adjective (\*last once / this last once, the same gap as for *few*; their referee's dissent in n. 26), larger phrases (*at least once*, *more than once*, *up to twice*, *the more than once which*), singulative and past (a lexical fact about *one* in both analyses), term of office (fusion's nominal with a fused modifier-head inside the attribute against a DP functioning as modifier; ordinary Head lighter). Verdict: the paradigm doesn't separate the analyses. §6's table gains the *once*/*twice*/*thrice* row above the line. Corpus awareness: the numerals paper (reynolds2026numerals) was already **deliberately distinguished** on 4 Oct, so §5 doesn't revisit it. Muse review running.

Singulative and past *once*: the determiner gap is the §4 default, which both analyses follow; the adjective gap is the trade already counted for *few* (fusion derives it, ordinary Head states it). 2026-10-18 — First commitment restated in §1 and §2 (Brett: "How does \"the noun heads the noun phrase\" survive?"). As worded it didn't: in *take some* there's no noun, and on both analyses *some*'s phrase heads the nominal. The commitment is the one that rules out the DP hypothesis, so it now reads "where a noun phrase has a noun, the noun is its head and the determinative a dependent" (§1), and "where an NP has a noun, the noun is its head, and the determinative is a dependent, as in *some apples*" (§2).

2026-10-18 — Nominal redefined by level, not position (Brett queried "the nominal, the part of its noun phrase below the determiner"). The old gloss was a tree metaphor and presupposed a determiner, which *take some* lacks on ordinary Head. CGEL: "The nominal is a unit intermediate between an NP and a noun" (p. 55); "Intermediate between the noun and the NP we recognise a category of nominals", with *old man* in *the old man* neither a noun nor an NP because it can't function as subject or object (p. 329). §1: "The phrase that *CGEL* fuses has one function, head of the nominal, as a common noun does in *take apples*. The nominal is the unit between noun and noun phrase, such as *old man* in *the old man*." §2 replaces "the layer of the NP below the determiner" with the same definition and CGEL's evidence (p. 329).

2026-10-18 — Wu's thesis (*Exocentric Noun Phrases in English*, UCL; title page 2020, UCL Discovery 2021) and ELL article (2020 online; 25(4), 825–849) read for the structural paper, via a read-only agent, with the key quotations checked by the parent (`reviews/wu-read-20261018-131422/` in the fusion repo; PDFs filed in `literature/` as `wu-2021-*`). Wu rejects fusion as a unified analysis ("FFT ... is not effective in explicating most of the ENPs that it claims to account for", p. 305) but keeps it, with reservations, for *once* (p. 97) and fused relatives (pp. 256–258, 305). He replaces it with an empty noun eN plus phrase-level feature assignment, after considering and rejecting determinative and adjective heads (Hypothesis 2, pp. 19–20; Quirk et al. and Lyons, pp. 114, 118: endocentricity, and number and genericity; "Adjective-head could be an option", p. 173). So Wu doesn't anticipate ordinary Head, by the agent's stated search (strings in the report). Corpus-awareness and prior-art adjudication: Wu **deliberately distinguished** (pending Brett's go-ahead on where to say so). Prior art found through Wu: Quirk et al. (1985: 421, §7.23) treat adjectives as "heads of noun phrases" that keep their category, which is ordinary Head's adjective half without the nominal level; to be acknowledged. Lyons (1991: 103), an "Adjective Head Rule" in a study of nationality terms, is not on disk.

Gisborne (2026, *JL*) verified to exist; PDF needed.

2026-10-18 — Decision 1 (Brett: "I don't see any alternative"): in the structural paper, peripheral *a* replaced. *A few* is a DP headed by *few*, with *a* as a modifier, and *a very few*, *a good few*, *a further few*, *a lucky few* are that DP with a modifier after *a* (§4, Figure 3 redrawn: *a lucky few* under fusion and ordinary Head, *a lucky few students*). Grounds: CGEL p. 385 (*a* in *a hundred days* is "an integral part of the numeral, and not as an independent determiner", since the NP is plural) extended by Reynolds 2026 (numerals, ELL: factors are modifiers of the magnitude, pp. 2, 10; interpolation, *one whole thousand*, p. 7). The numerals paper itself says nothing about *a*; the extension to *a few* is the structural paper's own step and is presented as such. Correction found while implementing: CGEL has a second route, *a* as determiner in sequences (*a further*, *a good*) that take a plural head quantified by a numeral or *few* (p. 353 [69]–[70], *A further few volunteers were needed*), and *a marvellous hundred days* with *a* as determiner (p. 385 n. 26). So the earlier claim that *a* "can't be the NP's determiner" was wrong against CGEL and is gone. §4 now gives both routes, shows that neither separates fusion from ordinary Head (the needed statement is shared), and adopts the DP analysis because it gives *a good few* one analysis where CGEL gives it two (pp. 353, 392). Consequence: *a lucky few* no longer counts against either analysis; ordinary Head's condition is "only after a determiner"; fusion's adjacency derivation stands with only *Poor everybody!* against it. Changed: §3 (adjective sentence, closing trade), §4, §6 table (adjective row; new shared row for *a few*) and trade paragraph, §1, abstract, §8. The *many a* footnote sentence was cut (it doesn't fit the DP analysis and isn't needed).

2026-10-18 — Decision 2 (Brett: "what would gelman do?"): the criterion is now a standard of evidence, not a gate. §2: "a property of form counts as evidence for a second function if it varies with that function and not with the word alone", motivated by the analyses' disagreeing only about structure and by CGEL's own use of form (the genitive, p. 420); a new paragraph says the criterion fixes what counts, not how much; the comparison is construction by construction (predictions, failures, statements, what the extra structure buys); no evidence is not evidence against; the standard applies to the paper's own additions (the *a few* DP rests on number; the fused-relative licences count against ordinary Head). §6: the bare-vs-after-determiner comparison changes two things at once, so a fact that separates the uses is predicted by fusion and by any condition mentioning a determiner; the pre-head adjective is credited as evidence for the determiner function; one fact with one exception (*Poor everybody!*, parallel to *Lucky you!* for CGEL's pronoun statement) against generality for *few* and a two-parent constituent in every NP headed by a bare determinative; "the balance favours ordinary Head, though it doesn't force it"; what would reverse it is stated; the saving is in where fusion applies, not in whether the grammar has it (GPT Pro). GLM's gate reading (the adjective condition can't count as evidence) declined: the gap is order and dependents varying with the determiner function, which is the kind of form §2 names, and §7 argues from the same kind of explanatory reuse. Downstream: §1's results and concession, the abstract ("I find no such property for the modifier function or in Payne et al.'s constructions, and one for the determiner function ... That one fact, which has an exception, doesn't outweigh the generality of treating the word as a head"), §3's closing, §7's opening, and §8 now say "favours, though not forced" for Det–Head too. The thesis "fusion is favoured only in fused relatives" stands. Decision owner: Brett (delegated by the question); drafted by Claude (Opus 5.5) after an advisor check; for Brett's read.

2026-10-18 — Corpus awareness, numerals paper (Reynolds 2026, ELL) **deliberately distinguished**: it uses CGEL's Det–Head fusion as given (*I bought twenty-two*, "a DP in fused-determiner–head function", p. 5; the partitive *two of the cats*, p. 15; n. 6 on p. 11, "a fused-head DP can appear in a genitive construction (e.g. *those two's story*)"). The structural paper argues against Det–Head fusion; ordinary Head would restate those analyses with the DP as head of the nominal. No change to the structural paper yet: under *JL* anonymity it can't flag the numerals paper as the author's. *Those two's story* is a genitive on a cardinal heading the nominal after a determiner, relevant to §4's genitive comparison (division follows the word); deferred to the full read. Also noted: the numerals paper quotes CGEL p. 385 as "integral part of the numerative"; CGEL has "numeral". The structural paper quotes CGEL directly.

2026-10-18 — Gisborne (2026, *JL* First View, "Mutual dependency, English *wh*-clauses and Word Grammar") **adopted** in §7 as a dependency-grammar convergence: mutual dependency "should be dispreferred if a non-mutual account is possible and offers a plausible and coherent analysis of the data" (p. 2); given up for dependent interrogatives, kept for free relatives, "the dependency variant of the 'fused relative' analysis in Huddleston & Pullum (2002: 1073–1074)" (p. 16). Footnote separates his "fused word" (Tesnière; one morpho-phonological word realizing two syntactic words, rejected for want of commutation evidence, p. 6) from fusion of functions. Bib entry in the fusion repo's references-local.bib. His acknowledgements thank Brett as a seminar organizer and special-issue editor; the structural paper doesn't mention that.

2026-10-18 — Corpus follow-ups to the external reviews, gathered by Brett by hand and labelled by Claude (Opus 5.5, one pass; raw lines, labels, scripts and manifest in the fusion repo's private `reviews/corpus-case-adj-20261018/`). Records: `corpus/exclusion_tests/2026-10-18-brett-supplied-whoever-whomever-gaps-and-matrix.md`; additions to `…-adj-compound-exclamatives.md` and `…-once-genitive-and-this-adj.md`; new `corpus/premodification/2026-10-18-brett-supplied-bare-adj-few.md`. Findings:
- Case, after a preposition and before a pronoun: nearly all tokens have a non-subject gap; embedded-subject gaps are rare (5 of 330 lines). Cleaning out those, predicatives and clause-subject tokens moves the whomever share from 49% to about 52%, so the paper's figure is conservative.
- Case, matrix function: sentence-initial fused relatives that are the main-clause subject with a non-subject gap take whomever at most 20% (8 against 33 in a capped whoever display), against about 52% after a preposition. This is the corpus cell §7 lacked for the matrix effect, which so far rested on CGEL's judgment. Shares are upper bounds until Brett gives the whoever totals.
- *Poor everybody!*: NOW and iWeb have 6 more exclamations, 4 closing a series after a name or noun, 2 independent (a quoted remark; a Carrie Underwood lyric). Real but marginal, nearly always *poor*.
- Bare adjective + *few*: COCA has only headlines and blog posts; iWeb has 102 tokens after a preposition, mostly *select few*, often before a noun; *only select few* is absent from COCA and in NOW mostly Indian (17 of 22 bare). The gap holds for edited standard English; web and Indian English treat *select few* as a unit.
- *A good hundred appear* (COCA, plural agreement) fits the DP analysis of *a lucky few*.
- *This last once* as a plain string (NOW, iWeb): no instance of the construction.
Proposed paper changes (§7 case passage and footnote; §4 footnotes on *Poor everybody!* and *select few*) await Brett's go-ahead and the whoever totals.

2026-10-18 — Structural paper, Brett's seven decisions on the second-round triage, all applied in one edit pass (fusion repo drafts, uncommitted). Decision owner: Brett; drafted by Claude (Opus 5.5). (1A) Verdicts graded: Mod–Head the clearest case against fusion; Det–Head a reasonable preference for ordinary Head against a real advantage for fusion; fused relatives the strongest case for fusion, with usage added to CGEL's judgments. §6 now states fusion's best case first, drops "one fact with one exception", and puts the reversal condition as further restrictions that the same adjacency would predict and ordinary Head could only list (the old wording, "a form a determinative has only when it stands alone", was already met by *none*, as Fable noted); §2 says a fact counts if the word alone doesn't explain it, and that what something else could also explain weighs less. (2) *Poor everybody!* downgraded: fusion needs some further treatment of the exclamation, ordinary Head extends CGEL's pronoun exception, and it no longer separates the analyses; "exception" removed from the abstract, §§1, 3, 6, 8; no conversion sentence. (3A) §4 answers Fable's correspondence point: fusion builds one direction of the link into the structure, and *the some* and *the both of us* break just that direction; the other direction is a list on both. (4a) Verb-object cells added to §7 as Table 2 (next entry). (5) §8's companion paragraph cut; the structural paper no longer mentions the classification of determinatives as nouns. 2026-10-18 — Structural paper: *another's* and *a little* added (Brett: "yes, add a little"; COCA searches by Brett). §3 lists *another's* beside *either's*, *neither's* and *each's* as a genitive of a determinative standing alone (COCA `-one another 's [nn*]`: 1,331 tokens, 798 in edited prose; CGEL mentions the genitive only for reciprocal *one another's*, pp. 428, 1502, checked in the PDF), in the dilemma sentence, the list fusion must exempt, and Table 1. §4 extends the DP analysis of *a few* to *a little*, "which CGEL treats with *a few* (p. 392)". 2026-10-18 — Structural paper §3: *not* and *hardly* with the compounds are now modifiers in the DP on ordinary Head, not peripheral modifiers of the whole NP (Brett: "not always, surely"; Codex had raised the same point in the restrictor consult). CGEL p. 431 makes them DP-internal modifiers of the determinative (*not many*, *hardly any*; checked in the PDF), and on ordinary Head the compound's DP heads the nominal, so they modify it there as they do in *hardly any writer present*; the old citation, pp. 430–431 for "peripheral", was off (peripheral modifiers are §13, pp. 436–437). Consequence: the two analyses group *hardly anyone present* alike, so the paragraph contrasting the groupings and the follow-up on peripheral scope are gone, and with them §2's footnote introducing "peripheral modifier", which nothing else used.

2026-10-18 — Structural paper: knock-on of putting *not*/*hardly* inside the DP (Brett: "this doesn't cause problems elsewhere?"). Ordinary Head's condition, worded "a determinative heading a nominal takes an internal pre-head dependent only when the NP has a determiner", now read as excluding *hardly anyone*. Restated of the nominal: "a nominal headed by a determinative takes a pre-head modifier only when the NP has a determiner (*hardly* in *hardly anyone* modifies the determinative, inside its DP)"; the same scope in §6 ("no pre-head modifier in a nominal headed by a bare determinative"; the reversal condition "not about pre-head modifiers in the nominal") and §3 ("one stated condition on pre-head modifiers in the nominal"); §3's paraphrase of CGEL p. 423 now has CGEL's "internal". Checked and unaffected: Table 1, §4's options for *Poor everybody!* ("a modifier of the whole NP"), the restrictor argument, the summaries.

2026-10-18 — Structural paper: brackets moved from the NP to the fused (or putatively fused) constituent in 17 examples (Brett: "in many cases, the brackets pick out the NP but not the fused constituent. For example, in 3a, is it the other two, or other, or two, or other two?"; CGEL p. 416: with *the other two*, "a cardinal numeral as head"). Now *the other [two]*, *the [many] who did*, *the lucky [few] who found …*, *The [rich]*, *the [very poor]*, *the [larger]*, *the [some]* (twice), *their [elderly]*, *the [sociology]*, *the [dog]*, *a [felony]*, *a [nylon]*, *no [stopping him]*, Wu's *For [some] who …*, *the last [twice]* (twice). Left as they were: the genitives (the genitive phrase is what's at issue), the DP examples (*[a lucky few]*, *[select few] lenses*, *[a mere few] feet*: the DP is the constituent), the NPs whose point is the missing determiner (*Asylum for [Lucky Few]*, *[Poor everyone]*), and the fused relatives, pending Brett.

2026-10-18 — Structural paper: brackets back on the NPs, with the fused constituent underlined (Brett: "or maybe keep the brackets and underline?"), CGEL's own convention ("The bracketed expressions here are NPs, and the underlined expressions combine the function of head with that of a dependent", p. 332, checked in the PDF). The 17 bracket moves of a few minutes earlier are reversed. A macro `\fu` (ulem's `\uline`, loaded with `normalem`) underlines the constituent that would be fused on CGEL's analysis in 46 places: Det–Head (*[some]*, *[Few] would dare*, *[Mine] are ready*, the genitives *[many's]* etc.), Mod–Head (*[the other two]*, *[the many who did]*, *[the lucky few who …]*, *[The rich]*, *[the very poor]*, *[the larger]*, *[the some]*, *[their elderly]*, *[the last twice]*), *nylon*-type nouns, and fused relatives (*[whatever he gave me]*, *[whichever of them comes up with the cash]*, *[in whichever city they want me to work]*, *[however responsive …]*). Not underlined where nothing is fused: *made of [nylon]*, *[Kim's] book*, *[no stopping him]*, *[partly nylon]*, predicative *[mine]*, the DPs as determiners (*[a mere few] feet*), the bare relatives (*[any of them comes up …]*). §1's footnote on judgment marks now states the convention.

2026-10-19 — Corpus record `corpus/exclusion_tests/2026-10-19-brett-supplied-every-of.md`. Brett pasted 67 COCA lines for *every of*. Partitive *every* alone is all but absent from edited contemporary prose: one doubtful *Esquire* line, apparently missing a noun. It does occur in unedited web text (19 lines), in speech slips (5), in older texts (14), and after *each and*, *any and*, or *all and*. Bare *every*, by contrast, has two edited NOW attestations (`every-reading.md`). This reverses Claude's suggestion from the muse triage to replace the starred partitive in §3 with a bare form. The partitive is the safer star. The open option is grounding it in CGEL's own example (\**Every of the apples was rotten*, p. 413). Paper unchanged pending Brett.

Held: option 2b, the polarity footnote. Brett's *scant* pair (*Scant attention has ever been paid to this problem*; *Scant attention was paid to this problem, was it?*) suggests that an internal modifier can make a clause negative. *Scant* is an attributive quantifying adjective for CGEL (p. 556) and is absent from its list of seven approximate negators (p. 815). That would undo CGEL's claim that "an internal modifier never can" (p. 394), and with it the only determiner-against-modifier argument the consults found. The footnote's wording is being revised.
Same day: corpus record `corpus/exclusion_tests/2026-10-19-brett-supplied-scant-ever.md`. COCA has 5 lines of *scant* with *ever*. In four, *ever* is inside the NP that *scant* modifies (*scant hope that … ever*). None has *ever* in the main clause. So clause-level negativity for *scant* rests on Brett's judgments; the 2b footnote stays held.
Same day: Brett is "not confident about the tag" in *Scant attention was paid to this problem, was it?*, and COCA shows *scant* licensing *ever* only inside its NP. 2026-10-19 — Structural paper §3, footnote 2b replaced at Brett's "replace the footnote". Clause negativity no longer gets the *nobody* and definite-determiner answer. Instead, the adjective *scant*, a modifier for CGEL (p. 556), patterns with determiner *few*:
- bare, it licenses *ever* in its clause (*scant regard was ever given to the people who lived there*, *Northern Daily Leader*, 2023);
- after a definite determiner, its clause takes *never* (*Ghana's scant resources "can never be safe"*, Myjoyonline, 2017).

Grounds: COCA, iWeb, and NOW collocates. 2026-10-21 — Fusion paper, GPT referee point 2 (interpretation), §2: keep *form* as the criterion, and give the reason interpretation drops out. Decision owner Brett, who accepted Claude's (Opus 5.5) recommendation of option A over widening the criterion (option B) or the minimum change (option C). Not yet applied.
- The sentence "Interpretation can't supply it, since the two assign the same readings, one by a second function, the other by what the context supplies" (analyses.tex:88) is replaced. It conflicted with §4, which already uses the generic reading against a silent noun.
- The replacement says that interpretation separates analyses that predict different readings, but fusion and ordinary Head predict the same readings, from the same sources.
- It cites CGEL's interpretive types, simple, partitive, and special (p. 333). They cross the functions: CGEL's examples put Det–Head in all three and Mod–Head in the partitive and the special. That *nylon* fits the simple type is the paper's application; CGEL p. 421 ties its reading to the modifier function.
- It ends with the test: a reading counts as evidence for a second function only if no independently needed source reaches it.
- Draft wording: fusion repo `reviews/gptpro-referee-20261021/INTERP-PROPOSAL.md`.

2026-10-21 — Fusion paper, GPT referee point 2: Warren (2006) is engaged as a silent-head analysis, not cited as support. Decision owner Brett, who chose option B without the Wu clause. Not yet applied.
- On a closer reading, prompted by Brett asking how *lunch box* "turns into a modifier", Warren gives metonyms an implicit head, because "it is the implicit element that agrees with the verb" (p. 77). She gives *the poor* the same structure, "the+ adjective+ implicit head" (p. 58). Her "double exposure" (p. 22) names exceptions, not her analysis. So the earlier recommendation, to cite her framing in the predicand paragraph, was withdrawn.
- One footnote goes on the silent-head sentence (analyses.tex:105). It cites Warren, then notes that (ex:dog)'s determiners follow the overt noun, then adds Nunberg 1995: 130 n. 18 (*une menthe*, gender follows *menthe*, not *sirop*). This replaces the separate Nunberg footnote proposed earlier.
- Wu 2025 isn't cited.

2026-10-21 — Fusion paper, GPT referee point 2, §4 and Table 1: *the rich*. Decision owner Brett, who took Claude's recommendations. Not yet applied.
- (a) Glass (2019) goes in the text at mod-head.tex:86, after PHP's generic point. *CGEL* p. 420 gives the same observation for *the rich people*. Glass adds individuated uses that aren't about people (*The weak and mutated die, the healthy survive*, p. 10) and abstract ones, singular like *CGEL*'s *the immoral* (p. 418) (*The crazy … is easily detected*, p. 7). She derives both readings from the adjective's own meaning (pp. 12–13).
  - It says "abstract" and "singular", not "non-count". Brett asked whether "non-count" was precise: a singular verb shows number, not countability, and *CGEL* describes the type as abstract and "obligatorily singular".
  - A footnote notes that her silent nominalizing head (p. 15) isn't needed on ordinary Head, since the AdjP heads a nominal.
- (b) mod-head.tex:98 says the determiners vary word by word, not type by type. *Unemployed*, which *CGEL* lists in the type, takes *many* and *some* in edited news; *these rich* and *some educated* occur mostly in blogs and in news outside Britain and the US. A footnote gives Brett's NOW and COCA counts (records 2026-10-21-brett-supplied-these-rich.md and -some-educated-many-unemployed.md) and Glass p. 7. Table 1's cell becomes "*the* (p. 417) or a genitive for *rich*, and also *many* or *some* for *unemployed*".
- (c) mod-head.tex:102: "is a list on both analyses" becomes "is stated the same way on both analyses", and the closing sentence becomes "Ordinary Head states the same, as permissions to head a nominal". This avoids a seeming clash with (b)'s "word by word" and with Glass's productivity claim (p. 11).

2026-10-21 — Fusion paper, GPT referee point 2, §3 (det-head.tex:24): von Fintel (1994) is cited for the semantic point only. Decision owner Brett, who took the revised option A without the footnote. Not yet applied.
- The domain of any quantified expression is supplied by the context, and it's needed even with a noun: Westerståhl's *most people* about Sweden (p. 41). The domain is an index on the quantifying word (p. 43), not a constituent, so it isn't a silent noun.
- With no noun, the context supplies the whole domain. That step is the paper's own.
- p. 45 isn't cited. Brett pointed out that von Fintel's "determiner-quantifier" (p. 3) is a word picked out by its kind of denotation, so his claim that determiners "require a common noun phrase argument" ("of a syntactic nature") is true by definition of CGEL's determiner function and false of its determinative category (*some*, *few* stand alone). The footnote replying to p. 45 was declined.
- The optional licence clause and the *few would* footnote addition aren't included. The (ex:dh)b line is cricket (Aaron Finch's side), so the addition couldn't have named Pakistan's people as drafted.

2026-10-21 — Fusion paper, GPT referee point 2: five refinements from muse's earlier triage (muse-wonder-full-1.0, written before the response began; Brett pasted it afterwards). Decision owner Brett, who took Claude's items 1–5. Applied; not committed.
1. §2: the test is now symmetric. "Each analysis still needs a route to the reading, and interpretation would distinguish them only where one needed a process the other doesn't." This replaces the one-way version.
2. §3: "With no noun, the context supplies all of it" becomes "the discourse, the predicate, or a conventional reading has to identify the set". This avoids muse's "whole restrictor" overclaim.
3. §4: a sentence on *the larger* (ex:mh f). The context supplies the alternatives and the comparative the property, and *the* picks out the piece that has it. The referee's follow-up named this case.
4. §3: a licence clause. The domain says nothing about which determinatives can stand alone; both analyses list them (Table 1).
5. §2: the dog relation is named, "(bearing a picture of one)".
Not adopted: muse's material-to-object transfer for *I'll take nylon* with Warren p. 59 as the resource. *Nylon*'s own meaning applies to a garment (*This shirt is nylon*). Warren's own cases change countability (*nylons*, *a glass*). And she analyses these metonyms as implicit heads (pp. 58, 77), which the new footnote already reports.
Word count after the five refinements: 16,331 by the strict count (+58).

2026-10-21 — Fusion paper: English only, and the evidence decides. (Brett.)
- The paper is about English. Finnish comes out of the §7 statement of Bresnan and Grimshaw's head–gap agreement condition. Old English stays only where it bears on English. B&G (pp. 373–374) show Old English free relatives taking the matrix case even when it clashes with the function inside the clause (their (182), 'to whatever occupation is most pleasing to him': dative from the matrix preposition, nominative gap), the same clash as (ex:case).
- The aim is to follow the data, not to make a case for ordinary Head. Claude's first recommendation for the §6 Det–Head result (keep "a reasonable preference" on the §2 burden) is withdrawn. The paper's own extent/kind concession (costs.tex:53) leaves the burden without support for a cost that is only one of extent. See the private triage addendum of 21 Oct.
- Cross-paper consistency: determinatives-as-nouns pairs the D-noun categorization with ordinary Head and says neither commitment requires the other. Whatever the fusion paper concludes on Det–Head, the sibling's §evidence must not claim more.
- Deferred (cross-paper, 21 Oct, later). The fusion paper's exclamation test (fusion repo `notes/2026-10-21-exclamation-test.md`) found the following. Bare *Lucky few!* and *Lucky both!* are out. *Poor everybody!*, *Poor somebody!*, and bare *Poor man!* are in (Brett's judgments; NOW and COCA). The results favour ordinary heads for the compound determinatives but cut against ordinary Head for simple determinatives standing alone. This paper's §evidence ("Ordinary Head, fusion, and modifier attachment") pairs D-noun with ordinary Head for independent *some*, so it will have to engage this once the fusion paper settles §6.

2026-10-22 — Fusion paper: the "pre-head complement" reading of CGEL's *\*the sociology* is set aside. (Brett.)
- Claude had noted that CGEL's example of a noun that won't fuse with the head (*\*Bill likes the linguistics lecturer, but I prefer the sociology*, p. 415) would be a pre-head complement by CGEL p. 439 (*a linguistics student*), and proposed testing whether complement nouns resist the reading supplied by the antecedent while modifier nouns allow it.
- Brett judged the pre-head complement argument weak. Payne 2025 (*Journal of Linguistics*, doi:10.1017/S0022226725100844) withdraws it: the dependents CGEL treats as pre-head complements should be "type-dependents", one class with the pre-head modifiers. Payne co-wrote CGEL ch. 5 and PHP 2007.
- The proposed test was also an anaphoric one, of the kind CGEL itself used for *?a criminal one* (p. 441) and that Payne 2025 sets aside as unreliable (citing Keizer 2004, 2011 and Payne, Pullum, Scholz, and Berlage 2013, neither read here). The analyses.tex paragraph on *nylon* and *the dog* stands as written.
- With no pre-head complements, "no fused complements" in English doesn't separate the analyses: apart from fused relatives, nothing after the head fuses with it, and post-head PPs and clauses can't head a nominal on either analysis.
- Open (Brett's call): whether to cite Payne 2025 in the fusion paper. It's cited nowhere in the portfolio. If it goes in, the entry goes in references-local.bib.

2026-10-23 — Fusion paper: correction to the 2026-10-22 entry on fused complements. (Claude, checked against sources after a GPT Pro critique that Brett pasted.)
- Wrong in that entry: "apart from fused relatives, nothing after the head fuses with it". PHP (p. 579) fuse the AdjP in *the very hard of hearing* as Head–Mod, and its counterpart follows the head (*the people very hard of hearing*). CGEL (p. 418) also allows fusion with a post-head modifier. The fusion draft already says both (mod-head.tex, the paragraph on PHP pp. 579–580 and its footnote).
- Wrong in Claude's chat reply of the same day, not in the log: that a PP can't head a nominal. PHP (p. 589 n. 31) take the PP *up to twice* as fused determiner–head. The fusion draft's costs table already lists it, with the same permission on ordinary Head (costs.tex, the row on change of category on the head path).
- Complement function as such doesn't block fusion. A genitive subject-determiner combines determiner with complement (subject) and "can also fuse with the head" (CGEL p. 56; *Max's attempt wasn't as good as Kim's*, p. 467 [41iii]). CGEL p. 56 calls *of the minister* in *the behaviour of the minister* "an internal complement and hence not a subject". The narrower claim that survives: internal complements don't fuse with the head. The one NP complement that does is the genitive subject-determiner, which is also a determiner. (The subject in a fused relative is the relative clause's subject, not a complement of the NP's head, so it's a separate case.)
- What fails is post-head PPs realising the head, whether complement or modifier (*those of Kim* but \**the of Kim*; *those in the corridor* but \**the in the corridor*; constructed). PHP (p. 569) say fusion constructions are "licensed by independent rules", so properties (i)–(vi) don't license \**the on the ring*; they just don't exclude it. Both analyses state the restriction as a positive permission by form. It still doesn't separate them.
- Reopened, Brett's call: whether the iWeb lines (*a few on the ring*, *few on what people feel*, *many about whether*) show complements licensed by the antecedent. A topic PP after a quantity word needs no antecedent noun (*not much about the election*), so these PPs may be modifiers, and the proposed test frames with an overt verb wouldn't tell the two apart. This bears on the pending det-head.tex sentence about complements licensed by an antecedent.
- Günther, if cited: the copy in literature/ is the 2018 Benjamins chapter (Linguistik Aktuell 246, pp. 77–112, doi:10.1075/la.246.04gun). On the definite article with a postmodifier and no noun, she reports Günther 2013's claim that the postmodifier can carry the contrast, says it makes the wrong predictions for silent nouns, and leaves the question open.

2026-10-23 — Fusion paper: a clausal complement after a fused determiner–head is attested. Update to the "Reopened" bullet in the correction entry above. (Claude checked the sources after a second GPT Pro reply that Brett pasted. Nothing decided.)
- Verified: Scottish Parliament, Public Petitions Committee, Official Report, 16 December 2008. After a member suggests closing a petition, the Convener says *We have a proposal to close the petition and another to keep it open.* CGEL (p. 1259, §8.2) lists *proposal* among nouns that license a to-infinitival complement. The parallel fixes the content reading, which rules out a purpose adjunct and an infinitival relative. Unlike the iWeb lines with *on* and *about*, this can't be a topic modifier, which can attach to any nominal. It extends CGEL's own complements after fused heads (*much of a husband*, p. 415; *Helen's of the Rachmaninov*, p. 421; *that of the queen*, p. 1515) from PP to clause. It's the candidate if the det-head.tex sentence on complements licensed by the antecedent goes in (Brett's call).
- Verified, different construction: Mandel, *New Left Review* I/169, has *a different question from the one whether* + clause. That's pro-nominal *one*, a noun, not a fused determinative. It counterexemplifies CGEL p. 1516 (content-clause complements "quite generally excluded" with that *one*; *\*The one that we hold a referendum*, p. 1515).
- Not verified, don't cite until read: Dancy, "An ethic of prima facie duties", in Singer (ed.), *A Companion to Ethics* (1991), p. 221, *another to keep our promises* (the chapter exists, but the phrase wasn't found). The Pete Edochie death-rumour report, *another that he was involved in a plane crash* (the page that could be reached has no such sentence). *Fore River Shipbuilding Co. v. Hagg* (1911), with *one* + interrogative (the page returned 403).
- The independent relative *whose* analogy doesn't make the PP-as-head gap interpretive. CGEL's *\*Their names weren't on the list of the dead, nor on the of the missing* (p. 1508) already has contrastive parallelism, with the PPs contrasting and the recovered noun constant. That's the environment Brett's *whose* paper names as licensing (papers/waiting/independent-relative-whose/main.tex, "Contrastive parallelism"), and the string still fails. (Claude's reading; Brett decides.)
- Corpus awareness, deferred: papers/waiting/independent-relative-whose. GPT Pro proposes probes combining independent relative *whose* with a retained complement (*I know an applicant whose to freeze fees was rejected*). They belong to the *whose* paper, not the fusion paper. CGEL p. 421 already gives the PP version with an ordinary genitive (*Helen's of the Rachmaninov*). Hankamer and Postal's starred examples followed an explicit mention of a gorilla; the *whose* paper already says so.

2026-10-23 — Fusion paper: a calibration footnote on the criterion (Brett: "add the calibration footnote"). It's in analyses.tex, at the end of the paragraph that states the criterion.
- CGEL uses form to decline extra structure for clausal subjects. A content clause functions as subject directly: it has the subject's position, tag, and agreement, but not subject–auxiliary inversion (*\*Doesn't that they haven't replied worry her?*, p. 956), and it isn't "sufficiently like an NP" to count as an object (p. 1018).
- The same tests find an NP in a bare determinative (*Did some leave?*, *with some*). So the criterion doesn't favour less structure as such.
- Anchored on CGEL's content-clause example and judgment. Brett's infinitival (*To be a linguist takes creativity*) is left out, because its inversion judgment would be his and isn't recorded.

2026-10-23 — Both papers: Juliette Cahard acknowledged (Brett: "add the Cahard acknowledgment to both papers").
- Where: the author \thanks footnote, after the contact address, in fusion-of-functions-revisited.tex and determinatives-as-nouns-taxonomic.tex. The combined manuscript determinatives-as-nouns.tex is the frozen source of the split, so it's untouched.
- Wording: thanks for "questions in correspondence in April 2026 about the head of a determinative standing alone". The fusion paper says they "prompted this work"; the taxonomic paper says "prompted the proposal made here".
- Grounds: Brett's private correspondence with Cahard in April 2026. It is summarized, with dates, in the fusion repo's private history (private/history-2026-10-23/), and is kept out of this public log.
- The acknowledgment comes out of anonymous review copies with the contact note. Brett may want to check with Cahard before publication.

2026-10-23 — Fusion paper: the calibration footnote revised after Brett's judgment that *Does to be a linguist take creativity?* is fine.
- Inversion was the footnote's main test, and it no longer works as a test of NP status. A *that* clause resists inversion (CGEL p. 956) but an infinitival doesn't, so the failure seems to go with *that*.
- The footnote now rests on CGEL's own grounds for not treating a content clause as an object (p. 1018). A content clause needn't follow the verb directly (*He denied categorically that…* but *\*He opened slowly the door*), and some verbs take it but no NP (*marvel*, *vouch*). A bare determinative patterns with the NP (*\*He took slowly some*, Claude's star, flagged for Brett; *I see some*, *with some*).
- The inversion contrast stays as the footnote's last sentence, with Brett's infinitival judgment.
- Brett confirmed the star on *\*He took slowly some* (2026-10-23), so it's his judgment under the paper's data note.

2026-10-23 — Fusion paper: an infinitival complement after a fused *some* is acceptable (Brett's judgment). *We have some proposals to close it and some to keep it open* is fine.
- Brett's 55 hand-gathered COCA lines of *some to* have no such complement. On Claude's reading:
  - 9 have *to* as a preposition (mostly Daniel 12:2);
  - 15 have *some* as the object of a catenative verb, and 4 as the subject of a *for*-infinitival;
  - 12 are gapped or supplementary;
  - 12 are infinitival relatives with a gap, so modifiers in the NP;
  - 2 are purpose infinitivals;
  - 1 is unclear and needs its context: the WashPost 1994 line, *some to assuage Western concerns*.
- So the absence is a matter of frequency, not grammar. A complement needs an antecedent noun that licenses it (CGEL p. 1259), and contexts with *some to* rarely supply one.
- Not yet recorded as corpus evidence or cited in the paper (Brett's call).
- Brett searched for an attested *some* + infinitival complement after a nominal with a licensing noun, and found none. He judges the constructed *We have some proposals to close it and some to keep it open* good (2026-10-23). Nothing has been added to the paper for it.

2026-10-23 — Fusion paper: the Det–Head verdict is that fusion is favoured for a simple determinative standing alone, though not forced. Decision owner: Brett ("go with C"); drafted by Claude (Opus 5.5).
- The options:
  - (A) keep "ordinary Head, a reasonable preference" on the burden of proof;
  - (B) unresolved, between ordinary Head and the intermediate grammar;
  - (C) favour fusion for simple determinatives standing alone.
- Why C:
  - The one property of form bearing on the determiner function, no adjective before a bare determinative (*\*Lucky few escaped*), points to fusion, and no property of form points the other way.
  - The exclamation test made that fact firmer. It also showed that ordinary Head's condition doesn't behave like the pronoun restriction it was modelled on (*Lucky you!*, *Poor man!*, but *\*Lucky few!*, *\*Lucky both!*).
  - Ordinary Head's advantage (fewer functions, two-parent structure in one construction) is a value, not evidence, and the cost it avoids is one of extent.
  - A rested on the argument withdrawn on 21 Oct. B presented a fact against a value as a standoff.
- Grading: fusion is favoured more firmly in fused relatives, where the head analysis needs statements no other head needs (fused-relatives.tex:126, still unverified per the 21 Oct addendum), than for determinatives, whose case rests on one fact that counts for less.
- Compounds: tentatively ordinary heads. They pattern with pronouns in the exclamation (*Poor everybody!*, *Poor somebody!*), on two COCA attestations and Brett's judgments.
- The grammar favoured is CGEL's without Mod–Head, with the compounds as ordinary heads.
- The verdict would reverse if a restriction that adjacency predicts for simple bare determinatives failed to hold, or if a property of form told against the determiner function.
- Edits:
  - abstract;
  - intro (the verdict paragraph, and "the one construction" gone);
  - analyses.tex:86;
  - det-head.tex:53 (the compounds in the exclamations);
  - mod-head.tex: the exclamation results, a footnote assuming no silent determiner (CGEL pp. 429, 430, 522–523), the compounds against fusion's derivation, and "looks like the pronouns' restriction but doesn't behave like it";
  - once.tex:31 (stale "kind the grammar already contains" removed);
  - costs.tex: Table 1 row, the trade paragraph, the verdict, the reversal conditions, and the favoured grammar;
  - conclusion.
- Compiles (make quick, 30 pp.). texcount sum 15,356, plus about 600 words of references, so about 950 over JL's 15,000.
- Deferred, cross-paper: the taxonomic paper's section "Ordinary Head, fusion, and modifier attachment" pairs determinatives-as-nouns with ordinary Head for independent *some*, and needs revising. analyses.tex:86 already says the noun classification removes the category change on both analyses, so the taxonomic thesis doesn't depend on ordinary Head.
- Deferred: the April 2026 payoff (getting rid of fusion for *take some*) is now reversed for simple determinatives. The private history records it.
- Follow-up edits the same day:
  - costs.tex: "nothing of form counts against" now points to §3, where the genitive divides the words in a way neither analysis predicts.
  - costs.tex: a third reversal condition, that the bare word is a modifier–head like CGEL's *blue* (p. 417), since adjacency then derives nothing. The verdict depends on the Det–Head choice, and det-head.tex:53 notes that CGEL gives no argument picking determiner over modifier for *many*. Open (Brett's call): whether to promote the polarity footnote (this file, 19 Oct) as the paper's argument for Det over Mod.
  - abstract: "simple" determinative.
  - mod-head.tex:169: the pronoun parallel holds "on this question" only.
  - mod-head.tex:109: the exclamations are the compounds'.

2026-10-23 — Fusion paper: the §7 claim that the head analysis needs "statements no other head needs" (fused-relatives.tex:126) checked against CGEL (Brett: "verify the claim"). It holds for standard English, with one footnote added.
- Subject relativization without a relative word. CGEL p. 1055 [68] has two less certain cases, in an it-cleft and an existential, "at the boundary between very informal and non-standard". CGEL ties them to position: the relative clause "could not be misconstrued as predicate of the matrix clause". A fused relative as subject is the misconstrual configuration, so these cases give the head analysis no parallel. Footnote added at fused-relatives.tex:64, closing the pending "note CGEL p. 1055 in §7".
- No infinitival form. CGEL §5 (pp. 1066–1067) restricts only the wh type (the relative phrase must be preposition + NP; no subject). The one head it reports as excluding a relative, *it* ([4ii]), excludes finite relatives as well. No head in CGEL's discussion takes a finite bare relative but not a non-wh infinitival one. Searched: CGEL ch. 12 §5, and the fusion paper's use of p. 1067. Not exhaustive beyond that.
- A case condition linking the head to a relative clause it isn't part of. CGEL p. 56: "Case is determined by the function of the NP in the larger construction". No parallel.

2026-10-23 — Fusion paper: why Det–Head and not Mod–Head for a bare determinative. Brett chose (b): the gap itself picks it, and the polarity footnote stays where it is.
- Correction: Claude had described promoting the polarity footnote (det-head.tex, the footnote opening "Polarity doesn't show it either") as the stronger fix. It isn't. The footnote shows that CGEL's clause-negativity argument (p. 394) doesn't separate determiner from modifier, given *scant*. Promoting it would move a doubt about the Det–Head choice into the main text.
- det-head.tex:53: CGEL gives no argument for *many*, "but the gap itself picks it". A bare modifier–head would leave room for an adjective before it in the nominal, as *the lucky few* has after its determiner, and only the determiner–head leaves none (*\*Poor many!*, Brett's judgment of 21 Oct). The text says this is one fact favouring one analysis over two rivals, not a second fact for fusion.
- costs.tex:59: the fact "counts for fusion with Det–Head, the analysis it also picks over Mod–Head".
- costs.tex:61: the third reversal condition is now "if independent evidence showed the bare word to be a modifier–head".

2026-10-23 — Fusion paper: back to the earlier verdict (Brett: "Let's go back to the earlier verdict"). Decision owner Brett; drafted by Claude.
- Brett's judgments: the two partitive attestations are good in context (*Lucky you. … lucky both of us actually*; *"Poor Ev," … Poor all of us*). Bare forms in the same context are not (*Lucky you. Lucky both!*, *Poor Ev. Poor all!*: "not so much"; marked * in the paper). *Poor many of us!*, *Lucky few of us!*, *Poor some of us!*, and *Poor all of the children!* need context before he can judge; not used.
- Verdict: ordinary Head is preferred for both functions, more clearly for the modifier than the determiner function, and not forced. Fusion stays only in fused relatives. This reverses this morning's verdict C. The reason is that the exclamation divides the fused determiner–heads among themselves: the compounds and partitives take the adjective, and simple determinatives standing alone don't. Fusion's adjacency is right for one kind and wrong for two, ordinary Head's exception is right for two and needs a limit for the third, and each needs one further statement. What's left is the ordinary pre-head gap, which fusion derives and ordinary Head states as the grammar states the pronouns' restriction. §6 now calls that saving and ordinary Head's fewer functions both economies of the grammar, not facts of form (GPT Pro's point), so the burden on the second function decides.
- Rewritten for the verdict: abstract (new, about 195 words, in place of cut C14), intro, §2's conditional, det-head.tex (the pre-head side is now "the only fact … that does", not "the one trace"; "the gap favours it", not "picks it"; partitives added to the exclamation exception), mod-head.tex's exclamation paragraphs (the four attestations, the bare forms starred, the whole-NP option made symmetric, and "favours neither"), Table 1's last row, §6's last three paragraphs, §7's whichever footnote and Figure 6's caption (both back to the earlier verdict's text), and the conclusion.
- Cuts applied, as the ones Claude and GPT Pro agreed on (Brett: "Make the cuts you and pro agree on"): C1, C2, C4–C7, C9, C11–C13, C16–C33, C35, C39, C39b, C40, C40b, C40c, C46, C51, C52 (C8 earlier).
